An introduction to V V -filtrations Thanks: Q.C. was partially supported by NSF grant DMS-1952399, B.D. was partly supported by NSF grant DMS-2001132 and NSF-MSPRF grant DMS-2303070, and M.M. was partially supported by NSF grants DMS-2301463 and DMS-1952399.
Qianyu Chen, Bradley Dirks, Mircea Mustaţă

Abstract
. We give an introduction to the theory of V V -filtrations of Malgrange and Kashiwara. After discussing the basic properties of this construction (in the case of a smooth hypersurface and, later, in the general case), we describe the connection with the theory of b b -functions. As an example, we treat the case of weighted homogeneous isolated singularities. We discuss the compatibility of V V -filtrations with proper push-forward and duality and the connection with nearby and vanishing cycles via the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence. We end by describing some invariants of singularities via the V V -filtration.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14F10, 32S40, 14B05

1.
Introduction

Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible complex algebraic variety. The sheaf
\mathcal{D}_{X}
of differential operators of
X
is the subsheaf of
{\mathbf{C}}
-algebras
of
{\mathcal{E}nd}_{\mathbf{C}}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and the sheaf of derivations
{\mathcal{D}er}_{\mathbf{C}}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
. A
\mathcal{D}
-module on
X

is simply a sheaf of (left or right)
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules. The theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules started being developed in the 70s in the work of Bernstein (in the algebraic setting,
when
X={\mathbf{A}}_{k}^{n}
, for any field
k
of characteristic
0
) and of Kashiwara and his collaborators (in the complex analytic setting, when
X
is a complex
manifold). While one of its original motivations came from the desire to have an algebraic approach to questions concerning systems of linear PDEs (hence the name

algebraic analysis
, sometimes used for this field), it turned out that the theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules had deep connections with other fields of mathematics, especially
representation theory and singularity theory. For details on the history of the subject and applications to representation theory, we refer to
[
HTT
]
.

A special feature of the field, that takes it outside of the usual algebro-geometric framework, is that
\mathcal{D}_{X}
is a sheaf of
noncommutative
rings. However,

\mathcal{D}_{X}
is not far from being commutative, and
coherent

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules turn out to have associated objects (depending on the choice of a suitable filtration)
that are coherent sheaves of
\mathcal{O}
-modules on the cotangent bundle

T^{*}X
(we review this briefly in Section
2.4
below). The
dimension
of a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module is defined by considering the
dimension of the support of the corresponding sheaf on
T^{*}X
(this support is, in fact, independent of any choices).
A fundamental result
in the theory says that the dimension of a nonzero coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module is always
\geq n=\dim(X)
. Because of this, the
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules
that are either zero or of dimension
n

(called
holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules) enjoy special properties (for example, they form an abelian category in which all objects have finite length). Furthermore, they
are preserved by natural pull-back and push-forward functors. The good properties of holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules are at the origin of many of the striking applications
of this theory.

One of the crown jewels of
\mathcal{D}
-module theory is the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence, proved independently by Kashiwara
[
KashiwaraRH
]
and Mebkhout

[
MebkhoutRH
]
. This gives an equivalence of categories
{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}\colon{\mathcal{D}}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
(the
analytic de Rham functor
). The left-hand side is the bounded derived category of complexes whose cohomology objects are regular holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules, while the right-hand side is the bounded derived category of complexes of
{\mathbf{C}}
-vector spaces on
X^{\rm an}
with constructible cohomology.
We discuss this briefly in Section
2.6
. This equivalence of triangulated categories induces an equivalence of abelian categories between regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules
and the famous category of perverse sheaves on
X^{\rm an}
, introduced by Beilinson, Bernstein, Deligne, and Gabber
[
BBDG
]
. A classical special case of this correspondence
(say, when
X
is projective) identifies the category of
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules on
X
that are
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-coherent (equivalently, these are vector bundles with integrable
connection) with the category of local systems on
X^{\rm an}
.

The role of the
V
-filtration is to give a description of two important constructions, the
nearby
and
vanishing cycles
, at the level of
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules.
More precisely, to any
{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
and any nonzero regular function
f\colon X\to{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
,
one can associate the nearby cycles
\psi_{f}{\mathcal{K}}
and the vanishing cycles

\varphi_{f}{\mathcal{K}}
in
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
. By a result of Gabber, if
{\mathcal{K}}
is a perverse sheaf, then both

\psi_{f}{\mathcal{K}}[-1]
and
\varphi_{f}{\mathcal{K}}[-1]
are perverse sheaves. If
{\mathcal{K}}={\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})
, for a regular holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, then the
V
-filtration provides a
\mathcal{D}
-module theoretic description of
\psi_{f}{\mathcal{K}}[-1]
and
\varphi_{f}{\mathcal{K}}[-1]
.
This construction was first introduced by Malgrange in
[
Malgrange
]
in the case when
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, with the goal of comparing two important
invariants of
f
, the roots of the Bernstein-Sato polynomial of
f
and the eigenvalues of the monodromy action on the cohomology of the Milnor fiber of
f
.
It was later generalized to arbitrary holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules by Kashiwara in
[
Kashiwara3
]
.

The
V
-filtration and its interplay with the Hodge filtration plays a key role in Saito’s theory of Hodge modules, see
[
Saito-MHP
]
and
[
Saito-MHM
]
.
First, the description of Hodge modules is done by induction on dimension and going from objects in dimension
n
to objects in dimension
n-1
is done
via the graded pieces with respect to the
V
-filtration. Second, Hodge modules come with a canonical filtration (the
Hodge filtration
) and this is required
to satisfy a suitable compatibility with the
V
-filtrations associated to arbitrary functions. It follows that in the setting of Hodge modules, it is important to keep track
of the
V
-filtration and the Hodge filtration at the same time. This is an important topic, but beyond the scope of these notes.

Another topic that we do not touch upon is the
V
-filtration associated to several functions. This more general version is also due to Kashiwara
[
Kashiwara3
]
,
but it is more subtle and less understood than in the special case of one function. While we do not treat the general case here for reasons of space, we refer to
[
BMS
]
for its
description and relation to
b
-functions
and to
[
CD
]
for its connection to the theory of Hodge modules.

Our goal here is to give an introduction
to
V
-filtrations. The following is an outline of what we cover.
In the next section we give an overview of some basic notions and results from the theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules that we need. In Section
3

we discuss the
V
-filtration on general
\mathcal{D}
-modules in the special case when the hypersurface defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
is smooth.
The case of an arbitrary function
f
is handled by a trick due to Kashiwara: if
\iota\colon X\hookrightarrow X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
is the closed immersion giving the
graph of
f
, then the
V
-filtration corresponding to a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is obtained by taking the
V
-filtration on the push-forward
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
with respect to the function

t
on
X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
coming from the coordinate on
{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
. We discuss this in Section
4
, while in
Section
5
we relate the
V
-filtration to
b
-functions. From this point on, the different sections can be read independently.
We give a nontrivial example of a
V
-filtration on
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
when
f

has weighted homogeneous isolated singularities in Section
6
. In Sections
7

and
8

we discuss
the compatibility of the
V
-filtration with duality and, respectively, proper push-forward, while in Section
9
we explain the description of the nearby
and vanishing cycles via
V
-filtrations. In the final Section
10
we discuss the connection between the
V
-filtration on

\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and certain invariants of singularities of the given hypersurface.

2.
A brief review of
\mathcal{D}
-module theory

In this section we review some basic definitions and results on
\mathcal{D}
-modules. For details and proofs we refer to
[
HTT
]
and
[
Bernstein
]
.
Unless explicitly mentioned otherwise, we work over an algebraically closed field
k
, of characteristic
0
.

2.1.
The sheaf of differential operators

Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible,
n
-dimensional algebraic variety over
k
. The
sheaf of differential operators
on
X
is the subsheaf of
k
-algebras

\mathcal{D}_{X}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}nd}_{k}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X}
(with
\mathcal{O}_{X}
acting on itself by multiplication) and the sheaf of
k
-derivations

{\mathcal{T}}_{X}={\mathcal{D}er}_{k}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
. If
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
form an algebraic system of coordinates on an open subset
U\subseteq X
(that is,

dx_{1},\ldots,dx_{n}
trivialize
\Omega_{U}
and we have the dual basis of
{\mathcal{T}}_{U}
given by
\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}
), then
\mathcal{D}_{X}|_{U}=\mathcal{D}_{U}

is a free left (or right)
\mathcal{O}_{U}
-module, with basis given by
\partial^{\alpha}:=\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}
for
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{n}
.

The sheaf
\mathcal{D}_{X}
carries an increasing filtration
(F_{p}\mathcal{D}_{X})_{p\geq 0}
by order of differential operators: if
U
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are as above,
then
F_{p}\mathcal{D}_{U}
is generated as a (left or right)
\mathcal{O}_{U}
-module by
\partial^{\alpha}
, with
|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}\leq p
.
It is easy to see that we have
F_{p}\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot F_{q}\mathcal{D}_{X}\subseteq F_{p+q}\mathcal{D}_{X}
and
[F_{p}\mathcal{D}_{X},F_{q}\mathcal{D}_{X}]\subseteq F_{p+q-1}\mathcal{D}_{X}
for all
p,q\geq 0
.
We may thus form the sheaf of graded rings
{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{D}_{X})=\bigoplus_{p\geq 0}F_{p}\mathcal{D}_{X}/F_{p-1}\mathcal{D}_{X}
and this is, in fact, a sheaf of

commutative
rings. Since
{\rm Gr}^{F}_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{X})={\mathcal{T}}_{X}
, it is clear that we have an induced morphism of sheaves of commutative rings

{\mathcal{S}ym}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{T}_{X})\to{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{D}_{X})

and by looking in local coordinates we see that this is an isomorphism. It is then easy to deduce that
for every affine open subset
U\subseteq X
, the ring
\mathcal{D}_{X}(U)
is both left and right Noetherian.

A left (right)
\mathcal{D}
-module on
X
is a sheaf of left (respectively, right)
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules.
Two basic examples of left
\mathcal{D}
-modules are
\mathcal{D}_{X}
and
\mathcal{O}_{X}
(note that we have a tautological action of
\mathcal{D}_{X}
on
\mathcal{O}_{X}
since
\mathcal{D}_{X}

is a subsheaf of
{\mathcal{E}nd}_{k}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
).

2.2.
The de Rham complex

Recall that a connection
\nabla
on an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is a
k
-linear map
\nabla\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Omega_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}
that satisfies the Leibniz rule:

\nabla(fu)=f\cdot\nabla(u)+df\otimes u
for every local sections
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
u\in\mathcal{M}
. Given such a connection
\nabla
, we get induced maps

\nabla\colon\Omega_{X}^{i}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}\to\Omega_{X}^{i+1}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}

that satisfy again a version of Leibniz rule. The connection is
integrable
if
\nabla\circ\nabla=0
on
\mathcal{M}
, in which case also the higher order compositions
vanish, so we have the de Rham complex

0\to\mathcal{M}\overset{\nabla}{\longrightarrow}\Omega_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}\overset{\nabla}{\longrightarrow}\ldots\overset{\nabla}{\longrightarrow}\Omega_{X}^{n}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}\to 0,

placed in degrees
0,\ldots,n
.
It follows easily from the definition that giving a left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module structure on
\mathcal{M}
is equivalent to giving an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module structure on
\mathcal{M}
,
together with an integrable connection. We denote by
{\rm DR}_{X}(\mathcal{M})
the corresponding shifted de Rham complex
\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}[n]
;
note that the maps in this complex are
not
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear. One can show (see
[
HTT
, Theorem 1.4.10]
) that a left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module is coherent as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module if and only if it is a locally
free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module of finite rank; in other words, it is a vector bundle with an integrable connection.

2.3.
The left-right equivalence

In what follows we will mostly consider left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules, but right
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules sometimes naturally enter the picture (for example, when considering duality
and direct image of
\mathcal{D}
-modules). A useful fact is that there is a canonical equivalence of categories between left and right
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules, as follows. If we denote
by
\mathcal{M}^{r}
the right
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module corresponding to the left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, then as
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules we have

\mathcal{M}^{r}=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\omega_{X}
, where
\omega_{X}=\Omega_{X}^{n}
, so
\mathcal{M}\simeq{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\omega_{X},\mathcal{M}^{r})
.
The right action of
\mathcal{D}_{X}
on
\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\omega_{X}
is easy to describe in a system
of coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, as follows. The map given by the
classical adjoint

(1)

P=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbf{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}}h_{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\mapsto{}^{t}P:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbf{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}}(-\partial_{x})^{\alpha}h_{\alpha}

is an automorphism
\mathcal{D}_{X}\simeq\mathcal{D}_{X}^{\rm op}
and if
\eta=dx_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}
, then

(m\otimes\eta)\cdot P=({}^{t}P\cdot m)\otimes\eta.

For example, the left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{D}_{X}^{\ell}
corresponding to the standard right
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module structure on
\mathcal{D}_{X}
is isomorphic to

\mathcal{D}_{X}
via the map given by the classical adjoint.
For the fact that in general we get a global equivalence of categories, see
[
HTT
, Section 1.2]
.

2.4.
Singular support and holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules

From now on, unless explicitly mentioned otherwise, all our
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules are left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules. Let
\mathcal{M}

be a
coherent
such
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module: this means that
\mathcal{M}
is quasi-coherent as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module and it is locally finitely
generated over
\mathcal{D}_{X}
. In this case one can show that there is a
good filtration
on
\mathcal{M}
, that is, an increasing filtration
(F_{p}\mathcal{M})_{p\in{\mathbf{Z}}}
with the following properties:

i)

F_{p}\mathcal{M}=0
for
p\ll 0
and
\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{p\in{\mathbf{Z}}}F_{p}\mathcal{M}
.

ii)

F_{p}\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot F_{q}\mathcal{M}\subseteq F_{p+q}\mathcal{M}
for all
p,q\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.

iii)

{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}):=\bigoplus_{p\in{\mathbf{Z}}}F_{p}\mathcal{M}/F_{p-1}\mathcal{M}
is locally finitely generated over
{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
.

Note that if
\pi\colon T^{*}X\to X
is the cotangent bundle of
X
, then we have a coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
T^{*}X
such that
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{F})\simeq{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
.
While
\mathcal{F}
depends on the choice of filtration, its support
{\rm Supp}(\mathcal{F})
(viewed as a subset of
T^{*}X
) is independent of the filtration.
This is the
characteristic variety
(or
singular support
)
{\rm SS}(\mathcal{M})
. The
dimension

\dim(\mathcal{M})
of
\mathcal{M}
is, by definition,
the dimension of
{\rm SS}(\mathcal{M})
. Note that
\dim(\mathcal{M})\leq\dim(T^{*}X)=2n
.

A fundamental result in the theory of
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules is
Bernstein’s inequality
: if
\mathcal{M}
is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module, then every irreducible component of
{\rm SS}(\mathcal{M})
has dimension
\geq n
.
In particular, if
\mathcal{M}\neq 0
, then

\dim(\mathcal{M})\geq n
.
A coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is
holonomic
if
\mathcal{M}=0
or
\dim(\mathcal{M})=n
.

Given a short exact sequence of coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules

(2)

0\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\to 0,

after choosing a good filtration on
\mathcal{M}
and taking the induced filtrations on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
,
we obtain the short exact sequence

(3)

0\to{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\to{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})\to{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime})\to 0,

so
\dim(\mathcal{M})=\max\big\{\dim(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}),\dim(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime})\big\}
. In particular,
\mathcal{M}
is holonomic if and only if both
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
are holonomic.
It is then easy to see that the holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules form an abelian category.

A more refined invariant than the characteristic variety is the characteristic cycle of a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module. Given a good filtration on the coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module

\mathcal{M}
, if
\mathcal{F}
is the coherent sheaf on
T^{*}X
corresponding to
{\rm Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
, the
characteristic cycle

{\rm CC}(\mathcal{M})
is

{\rm CC}(\mathcal{M}):=\sum_{Z}\ell(\mathcal{F}_{\eta_{Z}})[Z],

where the sum is over the irreducible components
Z
of
{\rm SS}(\mathcal{M})
and
\eta_{Z}
is the generic point of
Z
.
It is a bit more subtle, but one can show that the characteristic cycle, too, is independent of the choice of good filtration.
Given a short exact sequence (
2
) of holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
modules, we deduce from the corresponding exact sequence (
3
)
that
{\rm CC}(\mathcal{M})={\rm CC}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})+{\rm CC}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime})
. Since we clearly have
{\rm CC}(\mathcal{M})=0
if and only if
\mathcal{M}=0
, it is easy to deduce that
in the category of holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules, every object has finite length.

2.5.
Functors on
\mathcal{D}
-modules

We next recall the definition of pull-back and push-forward for
\mathcal{D}
-modules. Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of smooth, irreducible algebraic varieties over
k
.
If
\mathcal{M}
is a left
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module, then the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
f^{*}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes_{f^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})}f^{-1}(\mathcal{M})
has a natural structure of left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module:
the connection
\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}
on
\mathcal{M}
induces the following connection on
f^{*}(\mathcal{M})
:

\nabla_{f^{*}(\mathcal{M})}\colon f^{*}(\mathcal{M})\to f^{*}(\Omega_{Y})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}f^{*}(\mathcal{M})\to\Omega_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}f^{*}(\mathcal{M})

such that if
\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}(u)=\sum_{i}dg_{i}\otimes v_{i}
, then

\nabla_{f^{*}(\mathcal{M})}\big(h\otimes f^{-1}(u)\big)=dh\otimes f^{-1}(u)+\sum_{i}hd(g_{i}\circ f)\otimes f^{-1}(v_{i})\quad\text{for}\quad h\in\mathcal{O}_{X}.

We thus get a right-exact functor from
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-modules to
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules
that extends to an exact functor between triangulated categories
{\mathbf{L}}f^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
. Note that we denote by
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
, respectively
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{X}^{r})
, the bounded derived category of left, respectively right,

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules.

In particular, we see that
f^{*}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})
is a left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module; in fact, it is a
\mathcal{D}_{X}-f^{-1}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})
-bimodule, using also the right
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module structure of

\mathcal{D}_{Y}
. As such, it is denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}
.
It follows that if
\mathcal{M}
is a
right

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module, then
\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}
is a right
f^{-1}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})
-module, and thus

f_{*}(\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y})
is a right
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module. Since this construction combines a left exact functor with a right-exact functor, the correct functor
is defined at the level of derived categories. More precisely, we have the exact functor between triangulated categories

f_{+}={\mathbf{R}}f_{*}(-\otimes^{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y})\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{X}^{r})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{Y}^{r}).

Finally, the equivalence of categories between left and right
\mathcal{D}
-modules induces equivalences

\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{X}^{r})
and
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})\simeq\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{Y}^{r})
. Using these, we obtain the push-forward functor for left
\mathcal{D}
-modules

f_{+}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})
.

If
g\colon Y\to Z
is another such morphism, then we have a canonical isomorphism
(g\circ f)_{+}\simeq g_{+}\circ f_{+}
. Since every morphism
f\colon X\to Y

admits the decomposition
X\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}X\times Y\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}Y
, where
p
is the projection and
i
is the closed immersion
given by
i(x)=\big(x,f(x)\big)
, one can reduce understanding the push-forward via general morphisms to the case of projections and closed immersions.

We consider first the case of a closed immersion
i\colon X\hookrightarrow Y
between smooth varieties. Working locally, we may assume that we have
algebraic coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
on
Y
such that
X
is defined by
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
. In this case we have

\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}=\mathcal{D}_{Y}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})\mathcal{D}_{Y}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\mathcal{D}_{X}\partial_{y}^{\beta},

where the right multiplication by
y_{i}
and
\partial_{y_{i}}
are given by

P\partial_{y}^{\beta}\cdot\partial_{y_{i}}=P\partial_{y}^{\beta+e_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad P\partial_{y}^{\beta}\cdot y_{i}=\beta_{i}P\partial_{y}^{\beta-e_{i}}.

In particular, we see that
\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}
is a free left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module. Since both functors
i_{*}
and
-\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}
are exact,
we see that
i_{+}
is induced at the level of derived categories by an exact functor at the level of abelian categories.
An important result in this setting is Kashiwara’s Equivalence Theorem (see
[
HTT
, Section 1.6]
): this says that
i_{+}
gives an equivalence between the category
of coherent (holonomic)
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules and the category of coherent (respectively, holonomic)
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-modules with support contained in
X
;
the inverse functor is
{\mathbf{L}}i^{*}[-m]
. More generally, there is a natural isomorphism
i_{+}\circ{\mathbf{L}}i^{*}[-m]\simeq{\mathbf{R}}\Gamma_{X}

of functors on
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})
,
where
{\mathbf{R}}\Gamma_{X}
is the derived functor of the functor that maps
\mathcal{M}
to the subsheaf
\Gamma_{X}\mathcal{M}
of sections supported on
X
.
In particular, we have

(4)

i_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{H}^{m}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}),

where the right-hand side is the
m^{\rm th}
local cohomology sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
with respect to
X
.

Suppose now that
Y
and
Z
are smooth varieties and
p\colon X=Y\times Z\to Y
is the projection onto the first factor. In this case we have

\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}\simeq\mathcal{D}_{Y}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
.
It is easy to see that the de Rham complex
{\rm DR}_{Z}(\mathcal{D}_{Z})
is a complex of right
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
-modules and the corresponding
complex of left
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
-modules gives a
free resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. This implies that if
Y
is a point and if

\mathcal{M}
is a left
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
-module, then

p_{+}(\mathcal{M})={\mathbf{R}}\Gamma\big({\rm DR}_{Z}(\mathcal{M})\big),

so the cohomology of
p_{+}(\mathcal{M})
gives, up to a shift, the de Rham cohomology of
\mathcal{M}
. For arbitrary
Y
, we have

(5)

p_{+}(\mathcal{M})\simeq{\mathbf{R}}p_{*}\big({\rm DR}_{X/Y}(\mathcal{M})\big),

where
{\rm DR}_{X/Y}(\mathcal{M})
is the shifted relative de Rham complex
\Omega_{X/Y}^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}[\dim(Z)]
.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
denote the bounded derived category of complexes of
\mathcal{D}
-modules on
X
with holonomic cohomology.
It is an important result of the theory that pull-back and push-forward of
\mathcal{D}
-modules preserves holonomicity: if
f\colon X\to Y
is a morphism
of smooth, irreducible varieties, then we have induced functors

{\mathbf{L}}f^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\quad\text{and}\quad f_{+}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{Y}).

An important example is the following: suppose that
X
is the open subset of
Y
where
g\neq 0
, for some nonzero
g\in\mathcal{O}(Y)
. Note that
in this case
f_{+}
coincides with the usual push-forward functor at the level of
\mathcal{O}
-modules. We thus see that if
\mathcal{M}
is a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module,
then
f_{+}(\mathcal{M})=f_{*}(\mathcal{M})
is a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module. In particular, we see that
\mathcal{O}_{Y}[1/g]
is a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module (note that, a priori, it is not
even clear that this is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module). The key ingredient in proving the preservation of holonomicity by the push-forward via an open immersion
is the notion of
b
-function, that will play an important role also in Section
5
below.

Another important functor on holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules is provided by duality. More generally, we have a duality functor on the bounded derived category of left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules
with coherent cohomology
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
. Note that for every left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we have a right
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module structure on
{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{D}_{X})

induced by the canonical right
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module structure on
\mathcal{D}_{X}
, and thus a left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module structure on
{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{D}_{X})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\omega_{X}^{-1}
. We thus obtain a
duality functor

(6)

\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{D}_{X}),\quad\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{*}:={\mathbf{R}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{D}_{X})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\omega_{X}^{-1}[n],

where
n=\dim(X)
.
One can show that there is a natural isomorphism
(\mathcal{M}^{*})^{*}\simeq\mathcal{M}
; in particular, the functor
\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{*}
is an anti-equivalence of categories.
The reason for the shift by
n
in the definition of duality is due to the following two properties:
if
\mathcal{M}
is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module, then

i)

{\mathcal{E}xt}^{i}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{D}_{X})=0
for all
i>n
.

ii)

If
\mathcal{M}\neq 0
, then
\min\{i\geq 0\mid{\mathcal{E}xt}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}^{i}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{D}_{X})\neq 0\}=2n-\dim(\mathcal{M})
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{M}
is holonomic, we see that
{\mathcal{E}xt}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}^{i}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{D}_{X})=0
for
i\neq n
,
hence
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module; in fact, one can show that it is a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module. We thus get an induced anti-equivalence
of the abelian category of holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules and also an anti-equivalence of
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
.

2.6.
The Riemann-Hilbert correspondence

We now assume that
k={\mathbf{C}}
and write
X^{\rm an}
for the complex
manifold corresponding to the smooth complex algebraic variety
X
. Recall that a sheaf
\mathcal{F}
of
{\mathbf{C}}
-vector spaces on
X^{\rm an}
is
constructible
if
there is a finite decomposition
X=\bigsqcup_{i}X_{i}
, with each
X_{i}
being a locally closed algebraic subvariety,
such that the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to each
X_{i}
is a local system (that is, it is a locally constant sheaf of finite rank).
Let us write
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
for the bounded derived category of complexes of sheaves of
{\mathbf{C}}
-vector spaces on
X^{\rm an}
whose
cohomology sheaves are constructible. These categories satisfy a 6-functor formalism. In particular, we have the
Verdier duality
functor
{\mathbf{D}}_{X^{\rm an}}={\mathbf{R}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathbf{C}}\big(-,\underline{\mathbf{C}}_{X}[2n]\big)
on
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
and if
f\colon X\to Y
is a morphism of smooth, irreducible complex
algebraic varieties, we have exact functors

{\mathbf{R}}f_{*},{\mathbf{R}}f_{!}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(Y^{\rm an})\quad\text{and}\quad f^{-1},f^{!}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(Y^{\rm an})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})

that satisfy certain adjointness properties and compatibilities with duality.

Beilinson, Bernstein, Deligne, and Gabber defined in
[
BBDG
]
an abelian subcategory
{\rm Perv}(X^{\rm an})
of
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
whose
objects are known as
perverse sheaves
.
They are complexes with constructible cohomology that satisfy certain bounds on the dimensions of the supports of the cohomology sheaves
and whose Verdier duals satisfy the same conditions (in particular, the subcategory is preserved by Verdier duality).

If
\mathcal{M}
is a left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module, then we also consider the
analytification of the de Rham complex
{\rm DR}_{X}(\mathcal{M})
, that we denote by

{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})
. In his doctoral thesis, Kashiwara proved that
{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M})\in\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
.
In fact, he showed that
{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})
is a perverse sheaf, though this notion was formalized only later.

In order to obtain an equivalence of categories one needs to impose one extra condition:
regular singularities
. We do not give the definition since it is somewhat involved and we will not need it. For a thorough discussion, see
[
HTT
, Chapter 6]
.
If we denote by
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
the subcategory of
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
whose cohomology sheaves are regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules, then

\mathcal{D}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
is preserved by
{\mathbf{D}}_{X}
and
for a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
between smooth, irreducible complex algebraic varieties, we have induced functors

f_{+}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})\quad\text{and}\quad f^{\dagger}={\mathbf{L}}f^{*}[\dim(X)-\dim(Y)]\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{X}).

The Riemann-Hilbert correspondence, proved independently by Kashiwara and Mebkhout, says that the analytic de Rham functor
induces an equivalence of triangulated categories

{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}_{rh}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an}).

Moreover, via this equivalence, the functor
(-)^{*}
defined in (
6
) corresponds to
{\mathbf{D}}_{X^{\rm an}}
and if
f\colon X\to Y
is a morphism
of smooth, irreducible algebraic varieties, then
f_{+}
and
f^{\dagger}
correspond, respectively, to
{\mathbf{R}}f_{*}
and
f^{!}
.
Finally,
{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}
induces an equivalence between the abelian category of regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules and

{\rm Perv}(X^{\rm an})
. For a detailed discussion and for proofs, see
[
HTT
, Chapter 7]
.

3.
The
V
-filtration with respect to a smooth hypersurface

Our presentation of
V
-filtrations in this section is based on the one in
[
Saito-MHP
, Section 3.1]
.
Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible algebraic variety over
k

and let
t\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
be nonzero, defining a smooth, irreducible hypersurface
H
.
We put
\dim(X)=n+1
, so
\dim(H)=n
.

3.1.
The filtration
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{X}

For every
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, we put

V^{m}\mathcal{D}_{X}=\{P\in\mathcal{D}_{X}\mid P\cdot(t)^{q}\subseteq(t)^{q+m}\,\,\text{for all}\,\,q\in{\mathbf{Z}}\}

(with the convention that
(t)^{i}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
if
i\leq 0
).
It follows from the definition that we have
V^{m_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot V^{m_{2}}\mathcal{D}_{X}\subseteq V^{m_{1}+m_{2}}\mathcal{D}_{X}
for all
m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Note that the decreasing filtration
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{X}
only depends on
H
, but not on the function
t
defining
H
.

It is easy to describe
V^{m}\mathcal{D}_{X}
locally. Note first that if
U\subseteq X
is an open subset such that
H\cap U=\emptyset
, then
V^{m}\mathcal{D}_{X}|_{U}=\mathcal{D}_{U}
for all
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.
On the other hand, if
U
is an affine open subset with algebraic coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t
, then
given
P\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{D}_{X})
, we write
P=\sum_{\alpha,j}P_{\alpha,j}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{j}
, where
the sum is over
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{n}
and
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, and
all
P_{\alpha,j}
lie in
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
. In this case, it is straightforward to see that
P\in\Gamma(U,V^{m}\mathcal{D}_{X})
if and only if

P_{\alpha,j}\in(t^{m+j})
for all
\alpha
and
j
.

Given coordinates, as above, it is convenient to also describe
V^{m}\mathcal{D}_{X}
in terms of the Euler operator
t\partial_{t}
. In fact, for reasons that will become clearer later, we will use instead

s:=-\partial_{t}t=-t\partial_{t}-1
. For future reference, we collect in the next lemma some easy identities involving
s
,
t
, and
\partial_{t}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The operator
s=-\partial_{t}t
satisfies the following properties:

i)

For every
P\in k[s]
and every
m\geq 0
, we have

P(s)t^{m}=t^{m}P(s-m)
and
P(s)\partial_{t}^{m}=\partial_{t}^{m}P(s+m)
.

ii)

For every
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}
, we have

t^{m}\partial_{t}^{m}=(-1)^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}(s+j)\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}^{m}t^{m}=(-1)^{m}\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}(s-j).

Proof.

All assertions follow easily by induction on
m
.
∎

Using the above lemma, we see that
t^{i}\partial_{t}^{j}
lies in
k[s]\cdot t^{i-j}
if
i\geq j
and it lies in
k[s]\cdot\partial_{t}^{j-i}
if
i\leq j
.
We thus conclude that, given coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t
as above,

(7)

V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{U}=\mathcal{O}_{U}\langle\partial_{x_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x_{n}},s\rangle.

We also have

(8)

V^{m}\mathcal{D}_{U}=V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{U}\cdot t^{m}=t^{m}\cdot V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{U}\quad\text{for all}\quad m\geq 0\quad\text{and}

(9)

V^{-m}\mathcal{D}_{U}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{U}\cdot\partial_{t}^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\partial_{t}^{i}\cdot V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{U}\quad\text{for all}\quad m\geq 0.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that the operators
\partial_{t}
and
s=-\partial_{t}t
depend on the choice of coordinates.
More precisely, if we choose a different set of local coordinates
x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n},t
and write

\partial_{t}^{\prime}
for the corresponding derivation with respect to this system of coordinates, we have

\partial_{t}^{\prime}=\partial_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial^{\prime}_{t}(x_{i})\partial_{x_{i}}
, hence

\partial^{\prime}_{t}-\partial_{t}\in V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}\quad\text{and}-\partial_{t}^{\prime}t+\partial_{t}t\in V^{1}\mathcal{D}_{X}.

We also note that if we replace
t
by
t^{\prime}=ut
, for some invertible function
u
, then a similar computation shows that, with respect to the same coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
,
we have
\partial_{t^{\prime}}-\partial_{t}u^{-1}\in V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
and thus
\partial_{t^{\prime}}t^{\prime}-\partial_{t}t\in V^{1}\mathcal{D}_{X}
.

Remark 3.4
.

Note that all
V^{i}\mathcal{D}_{X}
are quasi-coherent sheaves of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules and for every affine open subset
U\subseteq X
, the ring
\Gamma(U,V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X})

is both left and right Noetherian. In fact, if we consider the
Rees-type sheaf of rings

{\mathcal{R}}_{+}(V^{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{X}):=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}V^{i}\mathcal{D}_{X}z^{i},

then
\Gamma\big(U,{\mathcal{R}}_{+}(V^{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{X})\big)
is both left and right Noetherian.
This is standard to check, using the filtration induced on
{\mathcal{R}}_{+}(V^{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{X})
by the order filtration on
\mathcal{D}_{X}
and showing that
the corresponding graded ring is Noetherian.

3.2.
The
V
-filtration with respect to
t

We now come to the definition of the
V
-filtration. The role of this filtration is, roughly speaking, to put the operator

\partial_{t}t
on a given
\mathcal{D}
-module in upper triangular form.

Definition 3.5
.

A
weak
1
1

1

This is not standard terminology. We will only use it in a few remarks in this section, in order to emphasize the different roles of the conditions in the definition of a
V
-filtration.

V
-filtration
on a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
(with respect to
t\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
) is a decreasing filtration

V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}=(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M})_{\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}}

by quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules,
indexed by rational numbers, which is exhaustive
2
2

2

This means that

\cup_{\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}}V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}
.
, discrete and left-continuous
3
3

3

These conditions mean that there is a positive integer
\ell
such that
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
takes a constant value
for
\alpha
in an interval of the form
\big(i/\ell,(i+1)/\ell\big]
, where
i
is any integer.
, and satisfies the following properties:

i)

We have
V^{i}\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\subseteq V^{\alpha+i}\mathcal{M}
for every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and
i\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.

ii)

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, the operator
(s+\alpha)
is nilpotent on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
.

We say that
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a
V
-filtration
if it satisfies, in addition, the following two conditions:

iii)

Each
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
is locally finitely generated over
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
.

iv)

We have
t\cdot V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}=V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
for all
\alpha>0
.

Note that, in the above definition, we put
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M}):=V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}/V^{>\alpha}\mathcal{M}
, where

V^{>\alpha}\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{\beta>\alpha}V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}=V^{\alpha+\epsilon}\mathcal{M}\quad\text{for}\quad 0<\epsilon\ll 1.

By assumption, there is a positive integer
\ell
such that
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})=0
unless
\ell\alpha\in{\mathbf{Z}}

(we will refer to this property by saying that the filtration is
discrete
).

Remark 3.6

(Restriction of
V
-filtration to an open subset)
.

It is clear from the definition that if
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a (weak)
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
with respect to
t
, then for every open subset

U\subseteq X
, the restriction
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}|_{U}
is a (weak)
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}|_{U}
with respect to
t|_{U}
.

Remark 3.7

(Behavior on
X\smallsetminus H
)
.

If
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a weak
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
, then
it follows from condition i) in Definition
3.5
that for every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, the quotient
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}/V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
is annihilated by
(t)
;
in fact, it is naturally a
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-module. In particular, each
{\rm Gr}^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathcal{M})
is naturally a
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-module.
Since the filtration is exhaustive and discrete, and
{\rm Gr}^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathcal{M})|_{X\smallsetminus H}=0
for all
\alpha
, we see that
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}|_{X\smallsetminus H}=\mathcal{M}|_{X\smallsetminus H}

for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Remark 3.8

(Independence of coordinates and of the equation
t
)
.

Since
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
is supported on
H
, we only put the condition in ii) in Definition
3.5
at the points in
H
;
note that the operator
s
is defined around these points. While this operator is only defined locally and depends on the choice of local coordinates,
its action on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
is well-defined: indeed,
as we have noticed in Remark
3.3
, if
s^{\prime}
is the same operator corresponding to a different choice of coordinates, then

s-s^{\prime}\in V^{1}\mathcal{D}_{X}
, hence the actions of
s
and
s^{\prime}
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
agree by condition i).

Furthermore, it follows from the same remark that if we replace
t
by
ut
, for some invertible function
u
, and if we denote by
s^{\prime}
the new operator, then
the actions of
s
and
s^{\prime}
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
again coincide. Therefore the notion of
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
only depends on
H
and it does not depend on the choice of function
t
.

Remark 3.9
.

In the presence of local coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t
, it follows from the formulas (
7
), (
8
) and (
9
) that the condition in i) in Definition
3.5
is equivalent to the fact
that each
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
is preserved by the action of
\mathcal{O}_{X}\langle\partial_{x_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x_{n}}\rangle
and

t\cdot V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\subseteq V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}\cdot V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\subseteq V^{\alpha-1}\mathcal{M}\quad\text{for all}\quad\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}.

Remark 3.10

(Coherence of
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
)
.

If
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
, then
it follows from condition iii) in the definition that each
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})

is locally finitely generated over
\mathcal{D}_{H}[s]
. Since
(s+\alpha)
is nilpotent by condition ii) in the definition, we conclude that
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
is a coherent

\mathcal{D}_{H}
-module.

Proposition 3.11
.

If
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a weak
V
-filtration on the coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, then for every
\alpha\neq 0
, the maps

(10)

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha+1}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\partial_{t}\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathcal{M})

are isomorphisms of
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-modules.

Proof.

Let
\nu_{t}
and
\nu_{\partial_{t}}
be the first, respectively the second, map in (
10
).
It is clear that they are morphisms of
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-modules, hence we only need to show that they are bijective.
It follows from condition ii) in Definition
3.5
that since
\alpha\neq 0
, the composition
\nu_{\partial_{t}}\circ\nu_{t}
is invertible,
hence
\nu_{t}
is injective and
\nu_{\partial_{t}}
is surjective. On the other hand, since
t\partial_{t}=\partial_{t}t-1
and
\alpha+1\neq 1
,
it follows from condition ii) in Definition
3.5
that
\nu_{t}\circ\nu_{\partial_{t}}
is invertible, hence
\nu_{t}
is surjective and
\nu_{\partial_{t}}

is injective. Therefore both
\nu_{t}
and
\nu_{\partial_{t}}
are bijective.
∎

It follows from the above proposition that the interesting maps are the following morphisms of
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-modules:

(11)

{\rm can}\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\partial_{t}\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{and}

(12)

{\rm Var}\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})

(the notation for these two maps is justified by the connection to topological vanishing and nearby cycles, see Remark
9.3
below).

Corollary 3.12
.

If
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a weak
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
, then the following hold:

If
\alpha\leq 0
and
u\in\mathcal{M}
is such that
tu\in V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
, then
u\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
. In particular, we have

\{u\in\mathcal{M}\mid tu=0\}\subseteq V^{0}\mathcal{M}.

If
\alpha\leq 1
and
v\in\mathcal{M}
is such that
\partial_{t}v\in V^{\alpha-1}\mathcal{M}
, then
v\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
.

For every positive integer
m
and every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
such that

-m\leq\alpha<-m+1
, we have

(13)

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}=\partial_{t}^{m}\cdot V^{\alpha+m}\mathcal{M}+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\partial_{t}^{j}\cdot V^{0}\mathcal{M}\quad\text{if}\quad-m\leq\alpha<-m+1.

Proof.

In the setting of i), since the
V
-filtration is exhaustive, it follows that there is
\beta\in{\mathbf{Q}}
such that
u\in V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}
. If
\beta\geq\alpha
, then we are done.
Otherwise, since
tu\in V^{>\beta+1}
and
\beta+1<\alpha+1\leq 1
, it follows from Proposition
3.11
that
u\in V^{>\beta}\mathcal{M}
. Since the
V
-filtration
is discrete, after repeating this finitely many steps, we conclude that
u\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
. The second assertion in i) follows by taking
\alpha=0
.

The argument for ii) is similar: let
\gamma
be such that
v\in V^{\gamma}\mathcal{M}
. If
\gamma\geq\alpha
, then we are done. Otherwise,
since
\partial_{t}v\in V^{>\gamma-1}\mathcal{M}
and
\gamma-1<\alpha-1\leq 0
, it follows from Proposition
3.11
that
v\in V^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M}
.
After repeating this finitely many steps, we conclude that
v\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
.

For the assertion in iii), we only need to prove the inclusion “
\subseteq
”. Since
\alpha<0
, for every
w\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
,
it follows from Proposition
3.11
that we can write

w=\partial_{t}w^{\prime}+w^{\prime\prime}
, where
w^{\prime}\in V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
and
w^{\prime\prime}\in V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}
, for some
\beta>\alpha
.
Since the filtration is discrete, we can iterate this argument to see that
w\in\partial_{t}\cdot V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}+V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
if
\alpha\leq-1

and
w\in\partial_{t}\cdot V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}+V^{0}\mathcal{M}
if
-1<\alpha<0
. The formula in (
13
) then follows by an easy induction on
m
.
∎

Note that the formula in (
13
) gives an explicit description of
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
for
\alpha<0
in terms of
V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}
, for
\beta\in[0,1)
.

Remark 3.13
.

If
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a weak filtration on the coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, then the following are equivalent:

a)

V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a
V
-filtration.

b)

The
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
V^{0}\mathcal{M}
is locally finitely generated and
t\cdot V^{i}\mathcal{M}=V^{i+1}\mathcal{M}
for
i\in{\mathbf{Z}}
,
i\gg 0
.

c)

The
{\mathcal{R}}_{+}(V^{\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{X})
-module
{\mathcal{R}}_{+}(V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}):=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}V^{i}\mathcal{M}z^{i}
is locally finitely generated.

We leave the proof as an exercise for the reader, since we will not need this fact.

Proposition 3.14
.

A coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
admits at most one
V
-filtration with respect to
t
.

Proof.

Suppose that
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
and
\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
are two
V
-filtrations on
\mathcal{M}
. By symmetry, it is enough to show that we have

(14)

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\subseteq\widetilde{V}^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\quad\text{for all}\quad\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}.

This is clear on
X\smallsetminus H
, hence we only need to check it around the points in
H
.
Note first that

(15)

\frac{V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}\cap\widetilde{V}^{\gamma}\mathcal{M}}{(V^{>\beta}\mathcal{M}\cap\widetilde{V}^{\gamma}\mathcal{M})+(V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}\cap\widetilde{V}^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M})}

is a subquotient of both
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\beta}(\mathcal{M})
and
{\rm Gr}^{\gamma}_{\widetilde{V}}(\mathcal{M})
. Property iv) in Definition
3.5
thus implies that
both
s+\beta
and
s+\gamma
are nilpotent on (
15
), and thus (
15
) is
0
for all

\beta,\gamma\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, with
\beta\neq\gamma
.

Let us fix now
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. Note first that since the filtration
\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is an exhaustive filtration by
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
-submodules
and
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
is locally finitely generated over
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
, there is
\gamma\in{\mathbf{Q}}
such that

(16)

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\subseteq\widetilde{V}^{\gamma}\mathcal{M}.

If
\gamma\geq\alpha
, then (
14
) holds and we are done, hence we may and will assume that
\gamma<\alpha
and that

\gamma
is maximal such that (
16
) holds, aiming for a contradiction (we may assume this since the filtration
\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is discrete and left continuous).
On the other hand, since
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
satisfies property iii), it follows that there is
m_{0}\geq 0
such that for every
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}
, we have

V^{\alpha+m_{0}+m}\mathcal{M}\subseteq t^{m}\cdot V^{\alpha+m_{0}}\mathcal{M}\subseteq t^{m}\cdot\widetilde{V}^{\gamma}\mathcal{M}\subseteq\widetilde{V}^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M}.

Since
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\not\subseteq\widetilde{V}^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M}
and the filtration
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is discrete and left continuous, it follows that there is
\beta\geq\alpha
such that

(17)

V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}\not\subseteq\widetilde{V}^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M}\quad\text{and}\quad

(18)

V^{>\beta}\mathcal{M}\subseteq\widetilde{V}^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M}.

Note that
\gamma<\beta
, hence the quotient
(
15
) for
\beta
and
\gamma
is
0
, and therefore

V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}\subseteq V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}\cap\widetilde{V}^{\gamma}\mathcal{M}\subseteq(V^{>\beta}\mathcal{M}\cap\widetilde{V}^{\gamma}\mathcal{M})+(V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}\cap V^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M})\subseteq V^{>\gamma}\mathcal{M},

where the first inclusion follows from (
16
), since
\beta\geq\alpha
, and the last inclusion follows from (
18
). However, this contradicts
(
17
), and thus completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

The proof of Proposition
3.14
shows that if
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
and
\widetilde{V}^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a weak
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
, then

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\subseteq\widetilde{V}^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Corollary 3.16
.

Given a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
and an open cover
X=\bigcup_{i}U_{i}
, then
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
if and only if the same holds for each

\mathcal{M}|_{U_{i}}
with respect to
t|_{U_{i}}
.

Proof.

Given a filtration
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{M}
it is clear that this is a
V
-filtration if and only if it induces a
V
-filtration on each
U_{i}
. The assertion in the corollary now follows from the fact that
by the proposition, given
V
-filtrations for each
\mathcal{M}|_{U_{i}}
, these glue to a filtration on
\mathcal{M}
.
∎

We next give two basic examples of explicit
V
-filtrations with respect to
t
.

Example 3.17

(
V
-filtration on a vector bundle with integrable connection)
.

If
\mathcal{M}
is a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module that is coherent as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module (hence locally free), then
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration given by

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}=(t)^{\lceil\alpha\rceil-1}\cdot\mathcal{M}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, with the convention that
(t)^{m}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
if
m\leq 0
. It is straightforward to check that the conditions in Definition
3.5

are satisfied. We only note that each
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
is clearly locally finitely generated over
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
since it is coherent as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module.
Also, condition ii) in the definition holds since if
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}
, then
(\partial_{t}t-m)t^{m-1}u=t^{m}\partial_{t}u\in V^{m+1}\mathcal{M}
, hence

(s+m)\cdot{\rm Gr}_{V}^{m}(\mathcal{M})=0
.

Example 3.18

(
V
-filtration on a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module supported on
H
)
.

If
\mathcal{N}
is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module supported on
H
, then
\mathcal{N}
has a
V
-filtration such that
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}=0
for all
\alpha>0

and
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{N})=0
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{<0}\smallsetminus{\mathbf{Z}}
. Indeed, note first that by Corollary
3.16
, in order to check this,
we may and will assume that
X
is affine and we have coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t
on
X
such that
H
is defined by
(t)
.
It is easy to check that if
\mathcal{N}_{i}=\{u\in\mathcal{N}\mid(s+i)u=0\}
, then the following hold
(these facts are steps in the proof of Kashiwara’s Equivalence Theorem, see
[
HTT
, Theorem 1.6.1]
):

i)

t\cdot\mathcal{N}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{i+1}
and
\partial_{t}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{i-1}
for all
i\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.

ii)

\mathcal{N}_{i}=0
for
i>0
and
\mathcal{N}=\bigoplus_{i\leq 0}\mathcal{N}_{i}
.

iii)

\mathcal{N}_{-m}=\partial_{t}^{m}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{0}
for all
m\geq 0
.

Since
\mathcal{N}
is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module, it follows that
\mathcal{N}_{0}
is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-module, and thus by iii) above, we see that
every
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{m}\mathcal{N}_{-i}
is a finitely generated
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module for every
m\geq 0
. It is then clear that we get a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{N}

by putting

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{-\lceil\alpha\rceil}\mathcal{N}_{-i}\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha\leq 0

and
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}=0
for
\alpha>0
.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module that has a
V
-filtration
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
with respect to
t
.

i)

For every
\alpha>0
, the map
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
is bijective.

ii)

If
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
and
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is the canonical projection, then
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
have
V
-filtrations
with respect to
t
given by

(19)

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}=p(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M})\quad\text{for all}\quad\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}.

Proof.

Suppose first that we are in the setting of ii) and we define the filtrations
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
as in (
19
).
Note that for every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have a short exact sequence

0\to{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\to{\rm Gr}^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathcal{M})\to{\rm Gr}^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime})\to 0.

It is straightforward to check that
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
is a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
. It is also clear that
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies all the conditions to make
it a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with the exception of condition iv) in Definition
3.5
(the fact that each
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is locally finitely generated over

V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
follows from the fact that
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
has this property and Remark
3.4
). However, this condition also follows if
we know that if
u\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
, with
\alpha>0
, is such that
tu\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, then
u\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
; in other words, it is enough to know that

\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies assertion i) in the proposition.

We now prove that every
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
that has a
V
-filtration satisfies the assertion in i).
We consider the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{M}_{1}\to\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{2}\to 0,

such that
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\Gamma_{H}(\mathcal{M})
. Note that in this case it is clear that if
tu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
, then
u\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
. The previous discussion thus implies that
the
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
induces
V
-filtrations on
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. On the other hand, since
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is supported on
H
, it follows from the uniqueness
of the
V
-filtration and Example
3.18

that
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1}=0
for all
\alpha>0
. Therefore
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\simeq V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}_{2}
for all
\alpha>0
, and since multiplication by
t
is injective on
\mathcal{M}_{2}
,
we see that multiplication by
t
is injective on
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
for all
\alpha>0
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

It
\varphi\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
is a morphism of coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules such that both
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
have
V
-filtrations with respect to
t
, then

\varphi
is a filtered morphism and it is strict
4
4

4

The fact that
\varphi
is a filtered morphism means that
\varphi(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M})\subseteq V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}

for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. The fact that it is strict means that, in addition, the filtration on
\varphi(\mathcal{M})
induced from
\mathcal{M}
is the same as the one induced from
\mathcal{N}
, that is,

\varphi(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M})=\varphi(\mathcal{M})\cap V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.
. In particular, the category of coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules that carry a
V
-filtration
with respect to
t
is an abelian category.

Proof.

It follows from assertion ii) in Proposition
3.19
that if we put

V^{\alpha}\varphi(\mathcal{M})=\varphi(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M})\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{V}^{\alpha}\varphi(\mathcal{M})=V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}\cap\varphi(\mathcal{M}),

then both these give a
V
-filtration on
\varphi(\mathcal{M})
. Therefore they agree by uniqueness of the
V
-filtration, hence
\varphi
is a filtered morphism and it is strict.
The last assertion in the corollary is an immediate consequence of this.
∎

The following proposition describes the behavior of
V
-filtration with respect to
\mathcal{D}
-module push-forward via closed immersions.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion map of a smooth, irreducible, closed subvariety such that
t|_{Z}
defines a smooth, irreducible hypersurface in
Z
.
If
\mathcal{N}
is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
-module and
\mathcal{M}=i_{+}(\mathcal{N})
, then
\mathcal{N}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t|_{Z}
if and only if
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
.
Moreover, in this case, if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{r},t
are local coordinates on
X
such that
Z
is defined by
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})
, so

\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathcal{N}\otimes_{k}k[\partial_{y_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{y_{r}}]
, then

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\simeq V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}\otimes_{k}k[\partial_{y_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{y_{r}}]\quad\text{for all}\quad\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
, then by assumption each
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
is preserved by the action of
y_{i}\partial_{y_{i}}
for all
i
.
This implies that if
\sum_{\beta}u_{\beta}\otimes\partial_{y}^{\beta}\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
, then
u_{\beta}\otimes\partial_{y}^{\beta}\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
for all
\beta
.
It is then straightforward to see that if we put
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}=\{u\mid u\otimes 1\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\}
for all
\alpha
, then
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{N}
is a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{N}
with respect to

t|_{Z}
. Conversely, if
\mathcal{N}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t|_{Z}
and we put
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}=V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}\otimes_{k}k[\partial_{y_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{y_{r}}]
,
it is straightforward to see that this is a
V
-filtration of
\mathcal{M}
with respect to
t
.
∎

3.3.
The maps
{\rm Var}
and
{\rm can}

We keep the same notation as before.

Proposition 3.22
.

If
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
and
i\colon H\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion, then

{\mathbf{L}}i^{*}(\mathcal{M})[-1]
is computed by the complex

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}),

placed in cohomological degrees
0
and
1
.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{>0}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\cdot}
\textstyle{V^{0}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\cdot}
\textstyle{{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\cdot}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{>1}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{1}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since the first vertical map is an isomorphism by Proposition
3.19
i), it follows that the second and third columns
are quasi-isomorphic. Therefore the first assertion in the proposition follows if we show that the inclusion of complexes

(20)

\textstyle{V^{0}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\cdot}
\textstyle{V^{1}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}}

is an isomorphism.

We first show that the induced morphism

\sigma\colon V^{1}\mathcal{M}/t\cdot V^{0}\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}/t\mathcal{M}
is an isomorphism.
It follows from Proposition
3.11
that if
u\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
, with
\alpha<1
, then there is

u^{\prime}\in V^{>\alpha}
such that
u-u^{\prime}\in t\mathcal{M}
. Since the
V
-filtration is discrete, after iterating this finitely many times, we see that
\overline{u}\in\mathcal{M}/t\mathcal{M}

lies in the image of
\sigma
. In order to prove that
\sigma
is injective, consider
u\in V^{1}\mathcal{M}\cap t\mathcal{M}
. If we write
u=tv
,
then it follows from Corollary
3.12
i) that

v\in V^{0}\mathcal{M}
, hence
u\in t\cdot V^{0}\mathcal{M}
. We have thus proved that the induced morphism between the cokernels of the vertical maps in (
20
)
is an isomorphism.

Finally, we need to show that the induced map

(21)

\{u\in V^{0}\mathcal{M}\mid tu=0\}\to\{u\in\mathcal{M}\mid tu=0\}

is an isomorphism.
This is clearly injective and surjectivity follows from
Corollary
3.12
i)
∎

Corollary 3.23
.

If
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
is a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}
has no
t
-torsion, then for every
u\in\mathcal{M}
and every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have
u\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}

if and only if
tu\in V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
. In particular, if the action of
t
on
\mathcal{M}
is invertible, then
V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}=t\cdot V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Proof.

For every
\beta\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, the map

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\beta}(\mathcal{M})\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\beta+1}(\mathcal{M})

is injective. Indeed, for
\beta\neq 0
this follows from Proposition
3.11
,
while for
\beta=0
it follows from the fact that
\mathcal{M}
has no
t
-torsion by Proposition
3.22
.
This implies that if
u\in V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}
is such that
tu\in V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{M}
for some
\alpha>\beta
, then
u\in V^{>\beta}\mathcal{M}
.
We thus get the first assertion in the corollary using the fact that the
V
-filtration is discrete. The last assertion in the statement
is an immediate consequence.
∎

Proposition 3.24
.

If the coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
and
i\colon H\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion, then the following hold:

i)

The
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-submodule
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot V^{>0}\mathcal{M}
is the smallest
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
with the property that
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is supported on
H
.

ii)

We have an isomorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-modules

\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\simeq i_{+}{\rm Coker}\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\partial_{t}\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\big).

Proof.

Given any
u\in\mathcal{M}
, if
u\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
and
m+\alpha>0
, then
t^{m}u\in V^{>0}\mathcal{M}
. Therefore
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is supported on
H
.
On the other hand, if
\mathcal{N}
is any
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{N}
is supported on
H
, then it follows from Example
3.18

and Proposition
3.19
that
V^{>0}\mathcal{M}=V^{>0}\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
, hence
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
.

In order to prove the isomorphism in ii), note first that since
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is supported on
H
, it follows from
Kashiwara’s Equivalence Theorem (see
[
HTT
, Theorem 1.6.1]
) and Example
3.18
that

\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\simeq i_{+}i^{\dagger}(\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\simeq i_{+}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\simeq i_{+}\big(V^{0}\mathcal{M}/(V^{>0}\mathcal{M}+(V^{0}\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))\big).

The assertion in ii) thus follows if we show that

(22)

V^{>0}\mathcal{M}+(V^{0}\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=V^{>0}\mathcal{M}+\partial_{t}\cdot V^{1}\mathcal{M}

(we may and will assume that we are in an open subset of
X
where we have coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t
on
X
, so that
we have the operator
\partial_{t}
acting on
\mathcal{M}
). The inclusion “
\supseteq
” in (
22
) is clear, hence we only
need to prove the reverse inclusion.
Note that since
V^{>0}\mathcal{M}
is a
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
, it follows that

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{m\geq 0}\partial_{t}^{m}\cdot V^{>0}\mathcal{M}.

Suppose now that
u\in V^{0}\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. We can write
u=\sum_{i=0}^{N}\partial_{t}^{i}u_{i}
, with all
u_{i}\in V^{>0}\mathcal{M}
.
If
w=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{t}^{i-1}u_{i}
, then
\partial_{t}w=u-u_{0}\in V^{0}\mathcal{M}
, hence
w\in V^{1}\mathcal{M}
by
Corollary
3.12
ii). Therefore
u=u_{0}+\partial_{t}w\in V^{>0}\mathcal{M}+\partial_{t}\cdot V^{1}\mathcal{M}
, which completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

4.
The
V
-filtration with respect to an arbitrary hypersurface

We next discuss the case when the hypersurface is not necessarily smooth. The idea, due to Kashiwara, is to construct the
V
-filtration on the push-forward
of a given
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module via the graph embedding.
In this section we assume that
X
is a smooth, irreducible,
n
-dimensional variety, and
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
is nonzero, defining the
(possibly singular) hypersurface
H
in
X
.

We consider the smooth, irreducible variety
X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
, of dimension
n+1
. We denote by
t
the coordinate on
{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
, defining
the smooth hypersurface
X\times\{0\}
. Consider the closed immersion
\iota=\iota_{f}\colon X\hookrightarrow X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
given by
\iota(x)=\big(x,f(x)\big)
.
Given a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we consider the coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}
-module
\iota_{+}\mathcal{M}
. With a slight abuse of terminology, we will
say that
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
if
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
.

In fact, we prefer to work with sheaves on
X
, rather than on
X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
. In other words, we will tacitly identify a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}
-module
\mathcal{F}

with the quasi-coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}\langle t,\partial_{t}\rangle
-module
p_{*}(\mathcal{F})
, where
p\colon X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}\to X
is the projection onto the first component.

Note that the formulas (
7
), (
8
), and (
9
) become in our setting

(23)

V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}=\mathcal{D}_{X}\langle s,t\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}\langle t,\partial_{t}\rangle,

(24)

V^{m}\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}=V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}\cdot t^{m}=t^{m}\cdot V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}\quad\text{for all}\quad m\geq 0\quad\text{and}

(25)

V^{-m}\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}\cdot\partial_{t}^{i}\quad\text{for all}\quad m\geq 0.

Let us describe explicitly
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
. We first consider the case when
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
Note that it follows from (
4
) that we have an isomorphism

(26)

B_{f}:=\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\iota(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}[t]_{f-t}/\mathcal{O}_{X}[t]

(recall that we view this as a sheaf of
\mathcal{D}_{X}\langle t,\partial_{t}\rangle
-modules on
X
).

We will denote by
\delta
the class of
\tfrac{1}{f-t}
in
B_{f}
.
Since
\mathcal{O}_{X}[t]_{f-t}/\mathcal{O}_{X}[t]=\bigoplus_{j\geq 1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\tfrac{1}{(f-t)^{j}}
and
\tfrac{(j-1)!}{(f-t)^{j}}=\partial_{t}^{j-1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{(f-t)}
,
we conclude that

B_{f}=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}\mathcal{O}_{X}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta.

The action of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\partial_{t}
with respect to this decomposition is clear, while the actions of
{\mathcal{D}er}_{k}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and of
t
are given by

(27)

D\cdot h\partial_{t}^{j}\delta=D(h)\partial_{t}^{j}\delta-hD(f)\partial_{t}^{j+1}\delta\quad\text{and}\quad t\cdot h\partial_{t}^{j}\delta=hf\partial_{t}^{j}\delta-jh\partial_{t}^{j-1}\delta

for every
D\in{\mathcal{D}er}_{k}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and
h\in\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Note now that if
\mathcal{M}
is an arbitrary coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module, then we have an isomorphism

\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})\simeq\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}B_{f}=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}\mathcal{M}\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta,

with the actions of
{\mathcal{D}er}_{k}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and of
t
being given by the analogues of the formulas in (
27
):

(28)

D\cdot(u\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta)=Du\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta-D(f)u\otimes\partial_{t}^{j+1}\delta\quad\text{and}\quad t\cdot(u\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta)=fu\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta-ju\otimes\partial_{t}^{j-1}\delta

for every
D\in{\mathcal{D}er}_{k}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and
u\in\mathcal{M}
.

Example 4.1
.

Besides
B_{f}
, one important example is that of

B_{f}^{\prime}:=\iota_{+}\big(\mathcal{O}_{X}[1/f]\big)=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}\mathcal{O}_{X}[1/f]\partial_{t}^{j}\delta.

Remark 4.2

(Behavior under restriction to an open subset)
.

If
U
is an open subset of
X
, we consider the restriction
g=f|_{U}
of
f
, and let
\iota_{f}\colon X\hookrightarrow X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
and
\iota_{g}\colon U\hookrightarrow U\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}

be the corresponding graph embeddings. It is then clear that for every coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we have a canonical isomorphism

(\iota_{g})_{+}(\mathcal{M}|_{U})\simeq(\iota_{f})_{+}(\mathcal{M})|_{U}

(recall that we consider both sides as sheaves on
U
). In this case it follows from Remark
3.6
that if
V^{\bullet}(\iota_{f})_{+}\mathcal{M}
is a
V
-filtration
on
(\iota_{f})_{+}(\mathcal{M})
, then
V^{\bullet}(\iota_{f})_{+}\mathcal{M}|_{U}
gives a
V
-filtration on
(\iota_{g})_{+}(\mathcal{M}|_{U})
.

Remark 4.3

(The support of
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
)
.

It follows from the definition that if
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
, then
t\cdot{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\iota_{+}\mathcal{M})=0
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.
A related fact is that as a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module,
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\iota_{+}\mathcal{M})
is supported on
H
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. Indeed, note that if
u\in V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})\cap\sum_{j\leq p}\mathcal{M}\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta
, then

fu=tu+(f-t)u\in V^{>\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})+\left(V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})\cap\sum_{j\leq p-1}\mathcal{M}\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\right).

We deduce arguing by induction on
p
that
f^{p+1}u\in V^{>\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
.

Remark 4.4

(Behavior when
\mathcal{M}
has no
f
-torsion)
.

It follows from the formula (
28
)
that in
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
we have

(29)

t\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{N}u_{i}\otimes\partial_{t}^{i}\delta=fu_{N}\otimes\partial_{t}^{N}\delta+(fu_{N-1}-Nu_{N})\otimes\partial_{t}^{N-1}\delta+\ldots+(fu_{0}-u_{1})\otimes\delta.

We deduce that multiplication by
t
on
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
is injective (or bijective) if and only if multiplication by
f
on
\mathcal{M}

is injective (respectively, bijective). We thus conclude using Corollary
3.23
that if
\mathcal{M}
admits a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
and
\mathcal{M}

has no
f
-torsion (or multiplication by
f
is invertible), then for every
u\in\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
and
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have
u\in V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})

if and only if
tu\in V^{\alpha+1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
(respectively, for every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have
t\cdot V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})=V^{\alpha+1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
).

Remark 4.5

(Restriction to the complement of
H
)
.

If
U=X\smallsetminus H
, then
\iota(U)
is contained in the complement of the hypersurface defined by
t
, hence it follows from
Remarks
3.7
and
4.2
that if
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
, then
V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})|_{U}=\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})|_{U}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. Moreover, if
f
is invertible (so
H=\emptyset
), then every
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
.

Example 4.6

(The case of a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module supported on
H
)
.

Note that
{\rm Supp}(\mathcal{M})\subseteq H
if and only if
{\rm Supp}\big(\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})\big)\subseteq X\times\{0\}
and in this case it follows from
Example
3.18
that
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
. Moreover, we have

V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})=0
for all
\alpha>0
and

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}\big(\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})\big)\simeq\big\{u\in\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})\mid t\cdot u=0\big\}

=\left\{\sum_{j\geq 0}\tfrac{f^{j}}{j!}u_{0}\otimes\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\mid u_{0}\in\mathcal{M}\right\},

where the last equality follows easily from formula (
29
)
(note that
f^{j}u=0
for
j\gg 0
by the assumption on
\mathcal{M}
).

Remark 4.7

(Compatibility of definitions smooth hypersurfaces)
.

We note that if
f
defines a smooth hypersurface in
X
, then the two notions of
V
-filtrations determine each other. More precisely, we have a
V
-filtration
on
\mathcal{M}
with respect to
f
if and only if we have a
V
-filtration on
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
with respect to
t
. Indeed, this follows from Proposition
3.21
, since

t\circ\iota=f
. Moreover, an easy computation using the proposition implies that the two
V
-filtrations determine each other as follows:
if we choose local coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},y
on
X
, with
y=f
, then

V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})=\sum_{j\geq 0}\partial_{y}^{j}\cdot(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\otimes\delta).

For example, if we take
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, then it follows from Example
3.17
that if the hypersurface defined by
f
is smooth, then the
V
-filtration on
B_{f}
is given by

V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot y^{\lceil\alpha\rceil-1}\delta=\sum_{j\geq 0}\mathcal{O}_{X}\cdot\partial_{y}^{j}y^{\lceil\alpha\rceil-1}\delta\quad\text{for all}\quad\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}},

with the convention that
y^{m}=1
if
m\leq 0
. In particular, we see that
V^{1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

Example 4.8

(The
V
-filtration on
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
when
H
is an SNC divisor)
.

For a more interesting example, let us consider the case of a simple normal crossing divisor. Suppose that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are algebraic coordinates on
X
, and

f=\prod_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}^{a_{i}}
, where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
are positive integers. For every
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, we put

I(f^{\lambda})=\big(x_{1}^{\lceil\lambda a_{1}\rceil-1}\cdots x_{r}^{\lceil\lambda a_{r}\rceil-1}),

with the convention that this is
\mathcal{O}_{X}
for
\lambda\leq 0

(with the terminology in Section
10.2
below, for every
\lambda>0
, this is the multiplier ideal
\mathcal{J}\big(X,(\lambda-\epsilon)H\big)
, where
0<\epsilon\ll 1
).
It is clear from the formula that
I(f^{\lambda_{1}})\subseteq I(f^{\lambda_{2}})
if
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}
.
Note also that we have
f\cdot I(f^{\lambda})\subseteq I(f^{\lambda+1})
, with equality
if
\lambda>0
.

Let us show that if we put

(30)

V^{\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\sum_{j\geq 0}\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot I(f^{\lambda+j})\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\quad\text{for all}\quad\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}},

then
V^{\bullet}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
.
A more general result is proved in
[
Saito-MHM
, Theorem 3.4]
, with a rather involved proof. We give a direct proof, by checking that the formula
in (
30
) satisfies the properties of the
V
-filtration. It is clear that this is a decreasing, exhaustive filtration. Note also that if
N={\rm lcm}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})
,
then
I(f^{\lambda})
is constant for
\lambda\in(i/N,(i+1)/N]
for every
i\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, hence the filtration we defined on
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
has the same property,
and thus it is discrete and left continuous.

Note first that the sum in the formula (
30
) can be replaced by a finite sum. Indeed, for every
i
, with
1\leq i\leq r
,
and every
b\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{r}
,
we have

(x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}-b_{i})\cdot x^{b}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta=-a_{i}x^{a+b}\partial_{t}^{j+1},

which implies

I(f^{\lambda+1})\partial_{t}^{j+1}\delta\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot I(f^{\lambda})\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\quad\text{for}\quad\lambda>0.

This implies that for
\lambda>0
we have
V^{\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot I(f^{\lambda})\delta
and, more generally, we have

V^{\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\sum_{j\leq\max\{1-\lambda,0\}}\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot I(f^{\lambda+j})\partial_{t}^{j}\quad\text{for all}\quad\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}.

This implies that every
V^{\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
is finitely generated over
\mathcal{D}_{X}
(hence also over
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}
).

Note next that for every
j\geq 0
, we have

t\cdot I(f^{\lambda+j})\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\subseteq I(f^{\lambda+j+1})\partial_{t}^{j}\delta+I(f^{\lambda})\partial_{t}^{j-1}\delta\subseteq V^{\lambda+1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X}),

so
t\cdot V^{\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\subseteq V^{\lambda+1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
for every
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. Moreover, this is an equality for
\lambda>0
. Indeed, we have seen that
in this case we have

V^{\lambda+1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot I(f^{\lambda+1})\delta=f\cdot I(f^{\lambda})\delta=t\cdot I(f^{\lambda})\delta=t\cdot V^{\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

Note also that we have

\partial_{t}\cdot I(f^{\lambda+j})\partial_{t}^{j}=I(f^{\lambda+j})\partial_{t}^{j+1}\delta\subseteq V^{\lambda-1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X}),

hence
\partial_{t}\cdot V^{\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\subseteq V^{\lambda-1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
for all
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.

In order to conclude, it is enough to show that if
b\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{r}
is such that
x^{b}\in I(f^{\lambda+j})
, then

(\partial_{t}t-\lambda)^{r}x^{b}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\in V^{>\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

Note that
x^{b}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\in V^{>\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
, unless there is
i
such that
b_{i}=(\lambda+j)a_{i}-1
.
In this case, if
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
is the standard basis of
{\mathbf{Z}}^{r}
, a simple computation gives

\partial_{x_{i}}\cdot x^{b+e_{i}}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta=(b_{i}+1)x^{b}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta-a_{i}x^{a+b}\partial_{t}^{j+1}\delta=-a_{i}(\partial_{t}t-\lambda)x^{b}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta.

We thus see that if
J=\big\{i\mid b_{i}=(\lambda+j)a_{i}-1\big\}
, then

(\partial_{t}t-\lambda)^{|J|}\cdot x^{b}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\in\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot x^{b^{\prime}}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta,

for some
b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{r}
such that
x^{b^{\prime}}\in I(f^{\lambda^{\prime}+j})
for some
\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda
.
We thus conclude that

(\partial_{t}t-\lambda)^{|J|}\cdot x^{b}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\in V^{>\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

5.
V
-filtrations and
b
-functions

The existence of
V
-filtrations is closely related to the existence of
b
-functions and the rationality of their roots. In this section we describe this relation.
As in the previous section, we assume that
X
is a smooth, irreducible variety over the algebraically closed
field
k
, of characteristic
0
. We consider a nonzero
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
that defines the hypersurface
H
and let
U=X\smallsetminus H
be its complement, with
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion. Let
\iota\colon X\to X\times\mathbf{A}^{1}
