and the de Rham complex
\Omega_{Z}^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}\mathcal{D}_{Z}
of
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
is quasi-isomorphic
to
\omega_{Z}[-d]
, it follows from the projection formula that

(63)

R^{j}p_{*}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}[d]\big)\simeq V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}R^{j}p_{*}\big(q^{*}(\omega_{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}\big),

where
q\colon X\to Z
is the projection onto the second component.
Since
p
is proper on the support of the coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
q^{*}(\omega_{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}
, it follows that

R^{j}p_{*}\big(q^{*}(\omega_{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}\big)
is a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module, and thus
(
63
) is a locally finitely generated
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module.
This completes the proof of Step 1.

We note that
p_{+}(\mathcal{M})

can be computed by the Godement resolution of the relative de Rham complex

\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}:=p_{*}\mathrm{God}^{\bullet}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}[d]\big).

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, we put

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}:=p_{*}\mathrm{God}^{\bullet}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}[d]\big)\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}.

It is clear that
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{N}^{j}
gives a decreasing, exhaustive, discrete and left-continuous filtration of
\mathcal{N}^{j}
for all
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.
Our goal is to show that
\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
is injective for all
j
and all
\alpha
and the images of these maps,
when we vary
\alpha
, give a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{H}^{j}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
with respect to
t
.

Note that the following
4
conditions hold for
(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},V^{\bullet})
:

(a)

For every
\alpha\in\mathbf{Q}
and
i\in\mathbf{Z}
, we have

V^{i}\mathcal{D}_{Y}\cdot V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\subseteq V^{\alpha+i}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}
, hence also

V^{i}\mathcal{D}_{Y}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha+i}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
for all
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
;

(b)

For every
\alpha\in\mathbf{Q}
, the operator
s+\alpha
is nilpotent on
{\mathrm{Gr}}_{V}^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}
;

(c)

For every
\alpha\in\mathbf{Q}_{>0}
, the left multiplication by
t\colon V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}
is bijective,
hence so is the left multiplication by
t\colon\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
for all
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
;

(d)

The
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
is locally finitely generated for all
\alpha\in\mathbf{Q}
and
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.

Indeed, conditions
(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
follow by functoriality of the Godement resolution from the definition of the
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}

and the condition
(d)
was proved in Step 1. If we show that for every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, the canonical morphism

\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})

is injective, then

\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})/\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{>\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\simeq\mathcal{H}^{j}({\mathrm{Gr}}_{V}^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})

and
(s+\alpha)
acts nilpotently on this by condition (b) above. Using also conditions
(a), (c), and (d) above, it is then clear that
\big(\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\big)_{\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}}
gives a
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{H}^{j}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})

with respect to
t
.

Step 2
. We prove a formal analogue of the injectivity of the maps
\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
for
\alpha\geq\beta
.
For every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, we put

\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}:=\varprojlim_{\gamma}V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet},

where the inverse limit is over
\{\gamma\in{\mathbf{Q}}\mid\gamma\geq\alpha\}
, with the standard order
(which is a directed set).
For every
\alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma
, we have an exact sequence of complexes

(64)

0\to V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to 0.

Since the maps in the inverse system
(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})_{\gamma}

are clearly surjective, the system satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition and by taking the inverse limit,
we get an exact sequence

0\to\widehat{V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}\to\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}\to V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to 0.

We conclude that for
\alpha\leq\beta
, the canonical morphism

(65)

\widehat{V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}\rightarrow\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}

is injective and we have a canonical isomorphism

(66)

V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\simeq\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}/\widehat{V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}.

The exact sequence (
64
) gives a long exact sequence

\cdots\to\mathcal{H}^{j-1}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\cdots.

A key observation is that
\delta
vanishes: note that
\delta
is compatible with the
k[s]
-action and we have polynomials
p
and
q
with roots in
(-\beta,-\alpha]

and
(-\gamma,-\beta]
, respectively, such that
p(s)
acts nilpotently on the domain of
\delta
and
q(s)
acts nilpotently on the codomain of
\delta
.
Since
p
and
q
are relatively prime, it follows that

\delta=0
. We conclude that for every
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, we have an exact sequence

(67)

0\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to 0.

In particular, the maps in the inverse system

\big(\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\big)_{\gamma}
are surjective. We can thus apply Remark
8.2
below to deduce that
we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{H}^{j}({\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}})\simeq\varprojlim_{\gamma}\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}).

Moreover, since the inverse system
\big(\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\big)_{\gamma}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition,
by taking the inverse limit of (
67
), we obtain the exact sequence

(68)

0\to\mathcal{H}^{i}(\widehat{V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})\to\mathcal{H}^{i}(\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})\to\mathcal{H}^{i}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to 0.

Step 3
. We now show
that the
t
-torsion subsheaf
\mathcal{T}^{j}_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
vanishes for
\alpha>0
. We prove this by descending induction on
j
.
The base case is trivial since
\mathcal{N}^{j}=0
for
j\gg 0
. Suppose now that
\mathcal{T}^{j+1}_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha>0
. The sequence

\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{t^{k}}\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\alpha+k}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to 0,

induced by the short exact sequence

0\to V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{t^{k}}V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\alpha+k}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to 0

is exact, since the image of the connecting map

\delta\colon\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\alpha+k}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j+1}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}),

is
t
-torsion and thus vanishes by the assumption that
\mathcal{T}^{j+1}_{\alpha}=0
. We conclude that

(69)

\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\alpha+k}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\simeq\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})/t^{k}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}).

We next show that for
k\gg 0
, the canonical map

\mathcal{T}^{j}_{\alpha}\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})/t^{k}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})

is injective, that is, we have
\big(t^{k}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\big)\cap\mathcal{T}^{j}_{\alpha}=0
. Indeed, since

\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
is a locally finitely generated
V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module, working locally we can find
a surjection
(V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{Y})^{N}\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
for some positive integer
N
.
For every
k\geq 0
, since
t^{k}\cdot V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{Y}=V^{k}\mathcal{D}_{Y}
, we deduce a surjection

(V^{k}\mathcal{D}_{Y})^{N}\to t^{k}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
.
We obtain a short exact sequence of graded modules over
{\mathcal{R}}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}V^{k}\mathcal{D}_{X}
:

0\to\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}Q_{k}\to{\mathcal{R}}^{N}\to\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}t^{k}\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to 0.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is generated over
\mathcal{R}_{0}=V^{0}\mathcal{D}_{X}
by
t\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
, it follows that
\mathcal{R}(U)
is both left and right Noetherian
(see Remark
3.4
);
therefore
\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}Q_{k}
is finitely generated over
\mathcal{R}
and, moreover, there is
k_{0}
such that
Q_{k+1}=t\cdot Q_{k}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
Since multiplication by
t
gives an isomorphism
V^{k}\mathcal{D}_{X}\to V^{k+1}\mathcal{D}_{X}
, we conclude by the Snake Lemma that multiplication by
t

gives an isomorphism
t^{k}\mathcal{H}^{i}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to t^{k+1}\mathcal{H}^{i}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})
for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Therefore we have

\big(t^{k}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\big)\cap\mathcal{T}^{j}_{\alpha}=0
for
k\geq k_{0}
, giving our assertion.

The composition

\mathcal{T}^{j}_{\alpha}\to\mathcal{H}^{j}({V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})

is injective as well for
\alpha>0
since (
67
) and (
69
) give canonical isomorphisms

\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})/\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha+k}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})\simeq\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\alpha+k}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\simeq\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})/t^{k}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}).

On the other hand, since
\alpha>0
, multiplication by
t
on
\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})}
factors as

\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})}\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha+1}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}}),

where the first map is an isomorphism and the second one is injective by (
68
). Therefore multiplication by
t
is injective on

\mathcal{H}^{j}(\widehat{V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}})
and we conclude that
\mathcal{T}^{j}_{\alpha}=0
.

Step 4
. Given
\alpha\leq\gamma
, if
\ell\gg 0
, then
\alpha+\ell\geq\gamma
, so that
t^{\ell}\cdot V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\subseteq V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}
. This implies that the kernel of the map

\delta^{j}\colon\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}),

is killed by
t^{\ell}
, hence it is contained in
\mathcal{T}^{j}_{\gamma}
. By
Step 3
, it follows that if
\gamma>0
, then all
\delta^{j}
are injective,
hence the exact sequence

0\to V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}\to 0

induces exact sequences

(70)

0\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to 0.

For every
\alpha\geq\beta
, by taking
\gamma>\max\{\alpha,0\}
, and combining (
70
) and
(
67
), we conclude that the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet}/V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to 0

is exact as well. This implies that

\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})\to\varinjlim_{\beta}\mathcal{H}^{j}(V^{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})=\mathcal{H}^{j}(\mathcal{N}^{\bullet})

is injective for every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and every
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
. As we have seen, this also implies the last assertion in the theorem.
∎

Remark 8.2

(A criterion for commuting cohomology and inverse limits)
.

Let
(A_{i}^{\bullet})_{i\in I}
be an inverse system of complexes, where
(I,\leq)
is a directed set. Suppose that for every
j\leq k
in
I
and every
q\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, the canonical maps

A_{k}^{\bullet}\to A_{j}^{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{H}^{q}(A_{k}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{H}^{q}(A_{j}^{\bullet})
are surjective.
Let
B_{i}^{m}\subseteq A_{i}^{m}
and
Z^{m}_{i}\subseteq A_{i}^{m}
be the coboundaries and, respectively, the cocycles.
Note that for every
j\leq k
in
I
, the induced map
B_{k}^{m}\to B_{j}^{m}
is clearly surjective. Moreover, we see that
Z^{m}_{k}\to Z^{m}_{j}

is surjective using the Snake Lemma and the exact sequences

(71)

0\to B_{i}^{m}\to Z_{i}^{m}\to\mathcal{H}^{m}(A^{\bullet}_{i})\to 0

for all
i\in I
. In particular, for every
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, the inverse systems
(B^{m}_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(Z^{m}_{i})_{i\in I}
satisfy the Mittag-Leffler condition.
Therefore the exact sequences (
71
) and

0\to Z^{m}_{i}\to A^{m}_{i}\to B^{m+1}_{i}\to 0

induce after taking inverse limits the exact sequences

0\to\varprojlim B_{i}^{m}\to\varprojlim_{i}Z^{m}_{i}\to\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{H}^{m}(A_{i}^{\bullet})\to 0

and

0\to\varprojlim_{i}Z^{m}_{i}\to\varprojlim_{i}A^{m}_{i}\to\varprojlim_{i}B^{m+1}_{i}\to 0.

We conclude from here that the canonical morphism

\mathcal{H}^{m}(\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}^{\bullet})\to\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{H}^{m}(A_{i}^{\bullet}).

is an isomorphism for all
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.

8.2.
The general case

Suppose now that
\pi\colon X\to Y
is any morphism between smooth, irreducible algebraic varieties
and
\mathcal{M}
is a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module such that
\pi
is proper on
the support of
\mathcal{M}
. Let
f\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)
be such that
g=f\circ\pi
is nonzero.
We assume that
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
g
and we want to show that in this case
each
\mathcal{H}^{j}(\pi_{+}\mathcal{M})
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
and to describe this
V
-filtration.

Let
\iota_{f}\colon Y\to Y\times\mathbf{A}^{1}
and
\iota_{g}\colon X\to X\times\mathbf{A}^{1}
be the corresponding graph embeddings, so we have the
Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathbf{A}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times\mathbf{A}^{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f}}

where
\pi^{\prime}=\pi\times{\rm Id}
.
Note that
\pi^{\prime}_{+}({\iota_{g}}_{+}\mathcal{M})\simeq{\iota_{f}}_{+}(\pi_{+}\mathcal{M})
and thus

{\iota_{f}}_{+}\mathcal{H}^{j}(\pi_{+}\mathcal{M})\simeq\mathcal{H}^{j}(\pi^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{N}\big),

where
\mathcal{N}={\iota_{g}}_{+}\mathcal{M}
. Let
t
denote the coordinate on
{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
.
We thus assume that
\mathcal{N}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
and we need to show that each
\mathcal{H}^{j}(\pi^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{N})
has a
V
-filtration
with respect to
t
and describe this
V
-filtration. As usual, we can factor
\pi^{\prime}
as

X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}X\times Y\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}Y\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1},

where
i
is the closed immersion given by
i(x,t)=\big(x,\pi(x),t\big)
and
p(x,y,t)=(y,t)
.
In this case, we can compute the
V
-filtration on
i_{+}\mathcal{N}
using Proposition
3.21
and we then compute the
filtration on
\mathcal{H}^{j}\big(p_{+}(i_{+}\mathcal{N})\big)\simeq\mathcal{H}^{j}(\pi^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{N})
using Theorem
8.1
for the smooth projection
p
.
Using this approach, we obtain the following

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to Y
be a morphism between smooth, irreducible algebraic varieties
and let
\mathcal{M}
be a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module such that
\pi
is proper on
the support of
\mathcal{M}
. If
f\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)
is such that
g=f\circ\pi
is nonzero and
\mathcal{M}
has a

V
-filtration with respect to
g
, then
\mathcal{H}^{j}(\pi_{+}\mathcal{M})
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
f

for every
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}
and we have isomorphisms

{\rm Gr}^{\alpha}_{V}\big(\mathcal{H}^{j}(\pi_{+}\mathcal{M})\big)\simeq\mathcal{H}^{j}\big(\pi_{+}{\rm Gr}^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathcal{M})\big)\quad\text{for all}\quad\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}.

Proof.

Using the previously described approach, we see that it is enough to prove the assertion separately in the following two cases:

i)

When
\pi
is a closed immersion and both
f
and
g
define smooth hypersurfaces. In this case the assertion follows from
the description of the
V
-filtration on
\pi_{+}(\mathcal{M})
in Proposition
3.21
.

ii)

When
\pi\colon Y\times Z\to Y
is the projection onto the first component. In this case, the assertion follows from Theorem
8.1
.

∎

9.
The comparison with the topological vanishing and nearby cycles

In this section we work over the ground field
k={\mathbf{C}}
. For every complex algebraic variety
Z
, we denote by
Z^{\rm an}
the corresponding analytic space.
Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible complex algebraic variety. Recall that in this setting we have the Riemann-Hilbert
correspondence provided by the analytic de Rham functor
{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(-)
.

9.1.
Topological vanishing and nearby cycles

For an introduction to the topological point of view on vanishing and nearby cycles, we refer to

[
Dimca
, Chapter 4.2]
and
[
Maxim
, Chapter 10.3]
(see also
[
Bry
, Section 1]
and
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
, Section 2]
). In what follows we simply review the basic definitions.

Let
f\colon X\to\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
be a regular function, which we view as a holomorphic map between complex manifolds
f\colon X^{\rm an}\to\mathbf{C}
.
Let
H=f^{-1}(0)
and
U=X\smallsetminus H
, with corresponding embeddings
i\colon H^{\rm an}\hookrightarrow X^{\rm an}

and
j\colon U^{\rm an}\hookrightarrow X^{\rm an}
.
Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}={\mathbf{C}}\smallsetminus\{0\}
be the punctured plane, so
U^{\rm an}=f^{-1}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})
. We have the following commutative diagram, with Cartesian squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\rm an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\rm an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\rm an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

where
\exp\colon\mathbf{C}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
is the universal cover of the punctured plane.

Following Grothendieck and Deligne
[
Deligne
]
, we define for
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
the
nearby cycles
complex
\psi_{f}\mathcal{K}
by

\psi_{f}\mathcal{K}:=i^{-1}\mathbf{R}(j\circ\chi)_{*}\chi^{-1}j^{-1}\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(H^{\rm an}).

The map
\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
, sending
z
to
z+1
, induces a
monodromy operator

T
on
\psi_{f}\mathcal{K}
. Moreover, adjunction for direct images gives a natural morphism
i^{-1}\mathcal{K}\to\psi_{f}\mathcal{K}
in
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(H^{\rm an})
. By definition, the
vanishing cycles

\varphi_{f}\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{K}
is the cone of this morphism, hence we have an exact triangle

(72)

i^{-1}\mathcal{K}\to\psi_{f}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{{\rm can}}\varphi_{f}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{+1}.

If
\mathcal{K}
is a perverse sheaf on
X
, then by a result of Gabber
[
Bry
, p. 14]
, both
\psi_{f}\mathcal{K}[-1]
and
\varphi_{f}\mathcal{K}[-1]
are perverse sheaves on
H
. We denote the shifted functor with a superscript
p
,
that is, we put

{}^{p}\psi_{f}:=\psi_{f}[-1]\quad\text{and}\quad{}^{p}\varphi_{f}:=\varphi_{f}[-1].

If we let
T
act on
i^{-1}\mathcal{K}
by the identity, then the first morphism in the triangle (
72
) commutes with
T
, hence we get a monodromy operator
T
on
\varphi_{f}\mathcal{K}
, too. As the category of
{\mathbf{C}}
-perverse sheaves is abelian and
{\mathbf{C}}
-linear, we can decompose these objects into generalized eigenspaces for the operator
T
, so we have

{}^{p}\psi_{f}\mathcal{K}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{*}}{}^{p}\psi_{f,\alpha}\mathcal{K},\quad{}^{p}\varphi_{f}\mathcal{K}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{*}}{}^{p}\varphi_{f,\alpha}\mathcal{K}.

By triviality of the monodromy on
i^{-1}\mathcal{K}[-1]
, we see that the morphism
{\rm can}
gives an isomorphism
{}^{p}\psi_{f,\alpha}\mathcal{K}\to{}^{p}\varphi_{f,\alpha}\mathcal{K}
for all
\alpha\neq 1
.

Remark 9.1

(The map
{\rm Var}
)
.

It is also possible to define a morphism
{\rm Var}\colon\varphi_{f,1}\mathcal{K}\to\psi_{f,1}\mathcal{K}
so that
T-1={\rm can}\circ{\rm Var}\colon\varphi_{f,1}\mathcal{K}\to\varphi_{f,1}\mathcal{K}
and
T-1={\rm Var}\circ{\rm can}\colon\psi_{f,1}\mathcal{K}\to\psi_{f,1}\mathcal{K}
.

9.2.
The vanishing and nearby cycles via the
V
-filtration

The following comparison theorem relates the vanishing and nearby cycles to the
V
-filtration on regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules.

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible complex algebraic variety and
H\subseteq X
a smooth hypersurface defined by
t\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. If
\mathcal{M}
is a regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module that has a
V
-filtration
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
with respect to
t
and

\mathcal{K}={\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M})
is the corresponding perverse sheaf, then there are natural isomorphisms

{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{\lambda}(\mathcal{M})\big)\cong{}^{p}\psi_{t,\alpha}\mathcal{K},\text{ where }\alpha=\exp(-2\pi i\lambda),\,\,\text{and}

{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\big)\cong{}^{p}\varphi_{t,1}\mathcal{K}.

Remark 9.3

(Compatibility with
{\rm can}
and
{\rm Var}
)
.

The above isomorphisms satisfy the additional property that the morphisms

{\rm can}\colon{}^{p}\psi_{t,1}\mathcal{K}\to{}^{p}\varphi_{t,1}\mathcal{K}

{\rm Var}\colon{}^{p}\varphi_{t,1}\mathcal{K}\to{}^{p}\psi_{t,1}\mathcal{K}

correspond under the functor
{\rm DR}_{H}^{\rm an}(-)
to the morphisms

-\partial_{t}\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\to{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})

\tfrac{\exp\big(2\pi i(s+1)\big)-1}{s+1}t\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\to{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}).

In particular, the factorizations
T-1={\rm can}\circ{\rm Var}
and
T-1={\rm Var}\circ{\rm can}
show that
T
corresponds to
\exp(2\pi is)
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})

and to
\exp\big(2\pi i(s+1)\big)
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})
. Similarly, for
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
\alpha={\rm exp}(-2\pi i\lambda)
, the action of
T
on
{}^{p}\psi_{t,\alpha}\mathcal{K}
corresponds to

{\rm exp}(2\pi is)
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\lambda}(\mathcal{M})
.

Remark 9.4

(The case of arbitrary hypersurfaces)
.

Suppose now that
X
is a smooth, irreducible complex algebraic variety and
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
is an arbitrary nonzero regular function defining the hypersurface

H\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}X
.
If
\iota\colon X\hookrightarrow X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}

is the graph embedding corresponding to
f
and
t
denotes the coordinate function on
{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
,
then it is easy to see that for every
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\rm an})
, we have

i_{*}(\psi_{f}\mathcal{K})\simeq\psi_{t}\big(\iota_{*}(\mathcal{K})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad i_{*}(\varphi_{f}\mathcal{K})\simeq\varphi_{t}\big(\iota_{*}(\mathcal{K})\big).

Suppose now that
\mathcal{K}={\rm DR}_{X}(\mathcal{M})
, for a regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
that admits a
V
-filtration with respect to
f
.
Since the functor
{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(-)
commutes with push-forward, we obtain from Theorem
9.2
a similar description of

{}^{p}\psi_{f,\alpha}\mathcal{K}
and
{}^{p}\varphi_{f,1}\mathcal{K}
in terms of the graded pieces of the
V
-filtration on
\iota_{+}(\mathcal{M})
.

We give a proof of Theorem
9.2
, following
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
]
(see also Wu’s survey article
[
LeiRH
]
). We refer also to
[
Saito-MHP
, Section 3.4]
for a proof of the comparison theorem for filtered
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules underlying mixed Hodge modules.

If
\mathcal{M}
is a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module, then we put
\mathcal{M}|_{H}:=\big(\mathbf{L}i^{*}(\mathcal{M}^{*})[-1]\big)^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{b}_{h}(\mathcal{D}_{H})
.
Note that if
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration
V^{\bullet}\mathcal{M}
with respect to
t
,
then Theorem
7.1
implies that
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, too, has such a
V
-filtration. By Proposition
3.22
, we see that

{\mathbf{L}}i^{*}(\mathcal{M}^{*})[-1]
is represented by the complex

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}^{*})\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}^{*}),

placed in degrees
0
and
1
, and we conclude using Theorem
7.1
that
\mathcal{M}|_{H}

is represented by the complex

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\partial_{t}\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}),

placed in degrees
-1
and
0
.

The general outline of the proof of Theorem
9.2
is the following: we wish to replace
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
with some objects whose behavior under the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence is better understood. This is done by the
moderate nearby cycles
construction (in Lemmas
9.8
and
9.9
), which essentially gives an isomorphism between
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
and the restriction of a certain
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module. In this step we also describe how
s
acts under this isomorphism.

This restriction is known, by
[
HTT
]
*Equation 7.1.5, to commute with the de Rham functor, yielding the usual restriction functor on the topological side. So the problem becomes understanding the moderate nearby cycles in a way which relates to the nearby/vanishing cycles of the de Rham complex of the module
\mathcal{M}
. This is done using various theorems from
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
]
, which we state below but do not prove. One technical point (seen in Theorem
9.11
) is that it is better at that point to work with the
solution complex
(defined below), which is dual to the de Rham complex.

The key fact underlying the theorems we use from
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
]
is that, for regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules, one can replace the solution complexes we are interested in with those of finite determination (see Theorem
9.12
). From there, after decomposing into the eigenspaces for the monodromy operator, one can even replace those solution complexes with those of
moderate growth
(as in Theorem
9.13
). These moderate growth solution complexes are precisely the de Rham complexes of the moderate nearby cycle construction, which completes the argument.

We begin by introducing some
\mathcal{D}
-modules that play an important role in the proof.
On
\mathbf{A}^{1}\smallsetminus\{0\}=\mathbf{G}_{m}
, for
\alpha\in(0,1]\cap{\mathbf{Q}}
and
k\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}
, we consider the regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-module
\mathcal{N}^{\alpha,k}:=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{G}_{m}}/\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{G}_{m}}(z\partial_{z}+\alpha-1)^{k+1}
, where we denote by
z
the coordinate on
{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
.
This module is free over
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}[1/z]
with generators
e_{\alpha,0},\dots,e_{\alpha,k}
,
where

e_{\alpha,\ell}=(z\partial_{z}+\alpha-1)^{k-\ell}\cdot\overline{1}\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leq\ell\leq k,

so that
(z\partial_{z}+\alpha-1)^{\ell+1}e_{\alpha,\ell}=0
.
If
j\colon\mathbf{G}_{m}\to\mathbf{A}^{1}
is the open embedding, then the
\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}
-module

\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k}:=j_{+}\mathcal{N}^{\alpha,k}
is again regular holonomic and has the same
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}[1/z]
-basis given by
e_{\alpha,0},\dots,e_{\alpha,k}
.

Remark 9.5

(The monodromy operator on
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k}
)
.

One can embed
\mathcal{N}^{\alpha,k}
in the
\mathcal{D}
-module consisting of the multivalued complex functions on
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
, such that

e_{\alpha,\ell}
maps to
\frac{1}{\ell!}z^{1-\alpha}\log(z)^{\ell}
for
0\leq\ell\leq k
. Note that the monodromy around a counter-clockwise loop around
0
in
{\mathbf{C}}
maps
\log(z)
to
\log(z)+2\pi i
. As
z^{1-\alpha}=\exp\big((1-\alpha)\log(z)\big)
, the monodromy acts on this multivalued function by multiplication with
\exp(-2\pi i\alpha)=:\xi_{\alpha}
.
Moreover, it acts on
\log(z)^{\ell}
by the formula

\log(z)^{\ell}\mapsto\big(\log(z)+2\pi i\big)^{\ell}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{j}(2\pi i)^{j}\log(z)^{\ell-j}.

Thus we can define a monodromy operator
T\colon\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k}\to\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k}
by the formula

e_{\alpha,\ell}\mapsto\xi_{\alpha}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\tfrac{(2\pi i)^{j}}{j!}e_{\alpha,\ell-j}\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leq\ell\leq k.

Note that this is a
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{A}^{1}}
-linear map.

We consider the
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
{\mathbf{L}}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k})=t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k})
, where we view
t\colon X\to\mathbf{A}^{1}
as a (flat) morphism of algebraic varieties.
For a regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we put

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}:=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k})=\bigoplus_{0\leq\ell\leq k}\mathcal{M}[1/t]e_{\alpha,\ell},

on which derivations act via the Leibniz rule (see
[
HTT
, Proposition 1.2.9]
). In particular, we have

(73)

(t\partial_{t}-1+\alpha)\cdot me_{\alpha,\ell}=(t\partial_{t}\cdot m)e_{\alpha,\ell}+me_{\alpha,\ell-1}\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leq\ell\leq k

(with the convention that
e_{\alpha,-1}=0
).
We have an induced automorphism
T\colon\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}
given by

T(m\otimes u)=m\otimes T(u)
.
This is
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-linear and thus induces automorphisms
\mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})\to\mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})
, for
i=-1
,
0
, that we also denote by
T
.

Remark 9.6

(Compatibity when we vary
k
)
.

Note that right multiplication by
(z\partial_{z}+\alpha-1)
induces, for every
\alpha
and
k
, a morphism of
\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}
-modules

\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k+1}
that maps
e_{\alpha,\ell}
to
e_{\alpha,\ell}
for all
0\leq\ell\leq k
. For every
\mathcal{M}
as above,
we thus get a corresponding morphism of
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k+1}
.

We note that if
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
, since
\coker\big(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)
also has one by Example
4.6
,
it follows that
\mathcal{M}[1/t]
has such a
V
-filtration by Proposition
3.19
and Corollary
5.20
.

Lemma 9.7
.

With the above notation, if
\mathcal{M}
has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
, then so does
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}
, and

(74)

V^{\beta}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}=\bigoplus_{0\leq\ell\leq k}V^{\beta+\alpha-1}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)e_{\alpha,\ell}\quad\text{for all}\quad\beta\in{\mathbf{Q}}.

Proof.

Using the formula (
73
) and the properties of the
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}[1/t]
, it is easy to check
that the filtration defined by the right-hand side of equation (
74
)
satisfies the properties of the

V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}
.
∎

Since
t
acts bijectively on
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}
, we know that
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})
is an isomorphism by Corollary
3.23
. Thus, we can identify

\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})=\ker\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})\overset{t\partial_{t}\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})\big).

Lemma 9.8
.

For every
\alpha\in(0,1]
and every
k
larger than the index of nilpotency of
(s+\alpha)
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
,
we have a functorial isomorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-modules

(75)

\varphi\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H}).

Moreover, we have
T\circ\varphi=\xi_{\alpha}\varphi\circ\exp\big(2\pi i(s+\alpha)\big)
, where
\xi_{\alpha}={\rm exp}(-2\pi i\alpha)
, and
we have
\varinjlim_{k}\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})=0
.

Proof.

Since
V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\to V^{\alpha}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)
is an isomorphism for
\alpha>0
(see Corollary
5.21
),
it follows from Lemma
9.7
that

V^{1}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}=\sum_{0\leq\ell\leq k}V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}e_{\alpha,\ell}.

Let
m_{0},\dots,m_{k}\in V^{\alpha}\mathcal{M}
.
Using (
73
), we obtain

t\partial_{t}\cdot\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}m_{\ell}e_{\alpha,\ell}=\big((t\partial_{t}-\alpha+1)m_{k}\big)e_{\alpha,k}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\big(m_{\ell+1}+(t\partial_{t}-\alpha+1)m_{\ell}\big)e_{\alpha,\ell}.

Since
t\partial_{t}-\alpha+1=-(s+\alpha)
, we conclude that

\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}m_{\ell}e_{\alpha,\ell}\in\ker(t\partial_{t})

if and only if
m_{\ell}=(s+\alpha)^{\ell}m_{0}
for
0\leq\ell\leq k
and
(s+\alpha)^{k+1}m_{0}=0
. Note that by our assumption on
k
, the latter condition is automatically satisfied.
We thus have an isomorphism

\varphi\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H}),\quad\varphi(m_{0})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\big((s+\alpha)^{\ell}m_{0}\big)e_{\alpha,\ell}.

In particular, we see that an element of
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})
is determined by the coefficient of
e_{\alpha,0}
.

Now, we wish to describe

T\colon\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})\to\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})
via the above isomorphism.
Let
m_{0}\in{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
, and consider

\varphi(m_{0})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(s+\alpha)^{\ell}m_{0}e_{\alpha,\ell}\in\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H}).

By definition of
T
, we have

T\big(\varphi(m_{0})\big)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(s+\alpha)^{\ell}m_{0}T(e_{\alpha,\ell}),

and, using the formula in Remark
9.5
, the coefficient of
e_{\alpha,0}
in this expression is

\xi_{\alpha}\cdot\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\tfrac{(s+\alpha)^{\ell}(2\pi i)^{\ell}}{\ell!}m_{0}=\xi_{\alpha}\exp\big(2\pi i(s+\alpha)\big)\cdot m_{0}.

In order to prove the vanishing
\varinjlim\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})
, we use again the fact that the map
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})
is an isomorphism; in particular, it is surjective. We thus have

\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})=\tfrac{{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})}{\partial_{t}\cdot{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})}={\rm coker}\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})\overset{\partial_{t}t}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k})\big).

We use the description of
V^{0}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}
in Lemma
9.7
. Note that
for every
k
, every
0\leq\ell\leq k
, and every

m\in V^{\alpha-1}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)
, it follows from (
73
) that

(\partial_{t}t)\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{j}\big((t\partial_{t}-\alpha+2)^{j}m\big)e_{\alpha,\ell+j}=me_{\alpha,\ell}+\big((s+\alpha-1)^{d}m\big)e_{\alpha,\ell+d}.

We thus conclude that
if
d
is larger than the nilpotency index of
(s+\alpha-1)
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha-1}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)
, then
the image of
me_{\alpha,\ell}
in

\varinjlim_{k}\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})
is
0
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In order to similarly describe
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})
, we first note that
\mathcal{N}_{1,0}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbf{A}}^{1}}[1/z]
, and thus for every
k\geq 0
,
we have an injective morphism of
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules
\mathcal{M}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1,k}
given by
m\mapsto me_{1,0}
. We get induced morphisms

\eta_{1}\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\to{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})
and
\eta_{0}\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\to{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})
, which are injective by Proposition
3.19
.
We consider the complex
(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}
, which we
define as the cone of the morphism
\mathcal{M}|_{H}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k}|_{H}
. Therefore this is the total complex of the double complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{t}}

that is, it is the following complex, placed in degrees
-2
,
-1
,
0
:

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{\partial_{t}\oplus\eta_{1}}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\oplus{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})\xrightarrow{-\eta_{0}+\partial_{t}}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k}).

The automorphism
T
on
\mathcal{M}_{1,k}
induces the identity on
\mathcal{M}
, hence the same holds on

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})
and
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})
. Therefore we can factor the map
T-{\rm Id}\colon(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\to(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}
as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\oplus{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\oplus{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{t}\oplus\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\eta_{0}+\partial_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T-{\rm Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T-{\rm Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\oplus{\rm Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{t}\oplus\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\eta_{0}+\partial_{t}}

We denote the top morphism of complexes in the diagram by

(76)

\widetilde{\rm var}\colon(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k}|_{H},

and the lower one by

(77)

\widetilde{\rm can}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1,k}|_{H}\to(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H},

so that
T-{\rm Id}=\widetilde{\rm can}\circ\widetilde{\rm var}
.

Lemma 9.9
.

With the above notation, the following hold:

i)

We have
\mathcal{H}^{j}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)=0
for
j\not\in\{-1,0\}
and
\varinjlim_{k}\mathcal{H}^{0}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)=0
.

ii)

If
k
is larger than the index of nilpotency of
(s+1)
on
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})
, then we have a functorial isomorphism

(78)

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{H}^{-1}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big).

iii)

Via the isomorphisms (
75
) and (
78
), the map

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\overset{-\partial_{t}}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})
corresponds to the natural morphism
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\widetilde{\rm can})

and the map
a\mapsto\tfrac{\exp(2\pi i(s+1))-1}{s+1}ta
corresponds to the
morphism
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\widetilde{\rm var})
.

Proof.

We have already observed that
\eta_{1}
is injective, hence
\mathcal{H}^{-2}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)=0
, which gives the first assertion in i).
The second assertion in i) follows from the exact sequence

\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k}|_{H})\to\mathcal{H}^{0}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)\to\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathcal{M}|_{H})=0

and the fact that
\varinjlim_{k}\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k}|_{H})=0
by Lemma
9.8
.

We next prove the assertion in ii), using the description of the
V
-filtration on
\mathcal{M}_{1,k}
in Lemma
9.7
.
Note that by (
73
), we have

\partial_{t}\cdot\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}m_{\ell}e_{1,\ell}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(\partial_{t}m_{\ell})e_{1,\ell}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\big(\tfrac{1}{t}m_{\ell+1}\big)e_{1,\ell}.

Given
m_{0},\dots,m_{k}\in V^{1}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)
and
a\in V^{0}\mathcal{M}
, the image of

\big(a,\sum_{\ell}m_{\ell}e_{1,\ell}\big)
in
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\oplus{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})

lies in the kernel of
-\eta_{0}+\partial_{t}
if and only if the following conditions hold:

-a+\partial_{t}m_{0}+\tfrac{1}{t}m_{1}=0\in V^{>0}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big),

\partial_{t}m_{1}+\tfrac{1}{t}m_{2}=0\in V^{>0}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big),\ldots,\partial_{t}m_{k}=0\in V^{>0}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big).

Since
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)\overset{t\cdot}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)
is injective, the above conditions are equivalent to

m_{1}=ta-t\partial_{t}m_{0}\mod V^{>1}\big(\mathcal{M}[1/t]\big)=V^{>1}\mathcal{M},

m_{2}=-t\partial_{t}m_{1}\mod V^{>1}\mathcal{M},\ldots,t\partial_{t}m_{k}=0\mod V^{>1}\mathcal{M}.

Suppose now that
(s+1)^{k}\cdot{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})=(t\partial_{t})^{k}{\cdot\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})=0
. We thus have an isomorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-modules

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\oplus{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{\overline{\Psi}}\ker(-\eta_{0}+\partial_{t})

(a,m_{0})\mapsto\left(a,\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\big((-t\partial_{t})^{\ell-1}ta\big)e_{1,\ell}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\big((-t\partial_{t})^{\ell}m_{0}\big)e_{1,\ell}\right).

Moreover, we see that

\overline{\Psi}(a-\partial_{t}m_{0},0)-\overline{\Psi}(a,m_{0})=\big(-\partial_{t}m_{0},-\eta_{1}(m_{0})\big)\in{\rm Im}(\partial_{t},\eta_{1}),

hence the map

\Psi\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathcal{H}^{-1}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big),\quad a\mapsto\overline{\Psi}(a,0)

is surjective. It is easily seen to be also injective, and thus it is an isomorphism.

We next turn to the assertions in iii).
Let
\varphi\colon{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k}|_{H})
be the isomorphism in Lemma
9.8
. Recall from the proof of that lemma that if

m_{0}\in{\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})
, then

\varphi(m_{0})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(-t\partial_{t})^{\ell}m_{0}e_{1,\ell}.

Since we have

\Psi(-\partial_{t}m_{0})=\left(-\partial_{t}m_{0},\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\big((-t\partial_{t})^{\ell}m_{0}\big)e_{1,\ell}\right)=\left(0,\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\big((-t\partial_{t})^{\ell}m_{0}\big)e_{1,\ell}\right)\mod{\rm Im}(\partial_{t},\eta_{1}),

we see that
\widetilde{\rm can}\big(\varphi(m_{0})\big)=\Psi(-\partial_{t}m_{0})
.

Finally, let
a\in{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}\mathcal{M}
. We have
\Psi(a)=\big(a,\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}((-t\partial_{t})^{\ell-1}ta)e_{1,\ell}\big)
and
\widetilde{\rm var}
maps this to the class of

(T-{\rm Id})\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(-t\partial_{t})^{\ell-1}tae_{1,\ell}\right)\in\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k}|_{H}).

We need to show that the coefficient of

e_{1,0}
is
\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\tfrac{(2\pi i)^{\ell}}{\ell!}(-t\partial_{t})^{\ell-1}ta
. This follows from the fact that by the formula in
Definition
9.5
and the fact that
\xi_{1}=1
, we have

(T-{\rm Id})e_{1,\ell}=\tfrac{(2\pi i)^{\ell}}{\ell!}e_{1,0}+\varepsilon,

where
\varepsilon
is a linear combination of
e_{1,j}
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 9.10
.

If
\mathcal{M}
is a regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module that has a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
, then the
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-module
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
is regular holonomic
for every
\alpha\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.11
, it is enough to prove the assertion for

\alpha\in[0,1]
. It is a basic fact that regular holonomicity is preserved under duality and pull-back (see
[
HTT
, Chapter 6]
). First, this implies that the tensor product
of regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules is regular holonomic, and thus all
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}
are regular holonomic. Second, it implies the fact that
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})

and
\mathcal{H}^{-1}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)
are regular holonomic and we conclude that
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})
is regular holonomic using
Lemmas
9.8
and
9.9
.
∎

For proof of Theorem
9.2
, we need to understand
{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})\big)
for a regular holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
that admits a
V
-filtration with respect to
t
. Note that
by exactness of direct limits, the vanishings and the isomorphisms in Lemma
9.8
and Lemma
9.9

give functorial quasi-isomorphisms

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{M})\to\varinjlim_{k}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,k}|_{H})\simeq\big(\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\varinjlim_{k}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k})\big)|_{H}\quad\text{ for }\quad\alpha\in(0,1],

and

{\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\to\varinjlim_{k}\big((\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)=\big(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\varinjlim_{k}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k})\big)|_{H},

hence we only need to understand the objects on the right hand side.

We note that the set-up for topological nearby cycles can also be used for the sheaf
\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}
. Define

\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}):=i^{-1}j_{*}\chi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{U}},

where
j\colon U^{\rm an}=\{t\neq 0\}\subseteq X^{\rm an}
is the open embedding and
\chi\colon\widetilde{U}\to U^{\rm an}
is induced
by the universal cover
\exp\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. By adjunction, we have an injective morphism
i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}\to\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
, whose cokernel we denote
\Phi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
.
We thus have an exact triangle in the derived category of
i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}
-modules

0\to i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}\to\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\xrightarrow{\rm can}\Phi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\to 0.

We also recall the
solution complex

{\rm Sol}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M}):=\mathbf{R}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}_{X^{\rm an}}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\rm an}})
associated to
\mathcal{M}
. This is related to the
analytic de Rham complex by duality: if
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is the dual of the regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, then we have by
[
HTT
, Sect. 4.6]
a canonical isomorphism

(79)

{\rm Sol}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M})\simeq{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M}^{*}).

This solution functor is more convenient thanks to the following theorem:

Theorem 9.11
.

\big(
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
, Thm 2.1-3]
\big)

For every coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we have a functorial isomorphism between the exact triangle

i^{-1}{\rm Sol}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M})\to\Psi_{t}{\rm Sol}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{\rm can}\Phi_{t}{\rm Sol}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{+1}

and the exact triangle

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Phi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\xrightarrow{+1}.

Proof.

By a standard argument, it suffices to construct a functorial morphism of triangles.
Indeed, then checking that this is an isomorphism can be done locally, and since
\mathcal{M}
is coherent, it is enough to check that
we have an isomorphism when
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{D}_{X}
, in which case the assertion is clear. Let us put
q=j\circ\chi
. Note that
q
is étale, hence the
\mathcal{D}
-module push-forward agrees with the
\mathcal{O}
-module push-forward.

To construct the morphism of triangles, we take a resolution of
\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}
by injective
\mathcal{D}_{X}^{\rm an}
-modules
\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}\to I^{\bullet}
. Then
q^{-1}I^{\bullet}
is a
q^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}
-injective resolution of
q^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}
and
q_{*}q^{-1}I^{\bullet}
is a
\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}
-module resolution of
q_{*}q^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}
. We then have functorial morphisms of triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},I^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{*}q^{-1}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},I^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Cone}(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},I^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(q_{*}q^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},q_{*}q^{-1}I^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Cone}(\alpha^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},I^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},q_{*}q^{-1}I^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Cone}(\beta)}
,
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

and thus a morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}q_{*}q^{-1}{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Cone}(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},q_{*}q^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Cone}(\beta)}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

By applying
i^{-1}
and using the functorial morphism

i^{-1}{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(-,-)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}(-),i^{-1}(-)\big),

we obtain the desired morphism of triangles.
∎

We can view the sections of
\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
as multi-valued holomorphic functions on
U^{\rm an}
. Consider the subsheaf
\Psi_{t}^{\rm fd}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})

of
\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm an})
consisting of those functions that are annihilated by some nonzero complex polynomial in the monodromy operator:
these are the functions of
finite determination
. This sheaf contains
i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}
, as monodromy acts trivially on regular functions.
We may thus consider the quotient denoted by
\Phi_{t}^{\rm fd}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
.

From the point of view of complexes of solutions, we may pass to functions of finite determination:

Theorem 9.12
.

\big(
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
, Thm. 3.1-4]
\big)

For every coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, the canonical morphisms

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Psi_{t}^{\rm fd}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\quad\text{and}

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Phi_{t}^{\rm fd}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Phi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)

are isomorphisms

By
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
, Thm. 3.1-2]
, a multi-valued holomorphic function is of finite determination if and only if it
can be written as a finite sum

\sum_{\alpha,j}c_{\alpha,j}t^{\alpha}\log(t)^{j},

where
\alpha\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
j\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, and each
c_{\alpha,j}
is a uni-valued function;
moreover, this expression is unique if we require the classes in
{\mathbf{C}}/{\mathbf{Z}}
of the
\alpha
with
c_{\alpha,j}\neq 0
to be pairwise distinct.
For any
\alpha\in{\mathbf{C}}
, we consider the subsheaf
\Psi_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
of
\Psi_{t}^{\rm fd}(\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm an})
consisting of those sections of the form

\sum_{j\geq 0}c_{j}t^{1-\alpha}\log(t)^{j}
for some fixed
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}
, with the
c_{j}
uni-valued.
In what follows we fix a section
\sigma\colon\mathbf{C}/\mathbf{Z}\to\mathbf{C}
of the canonical projection such that
\sigma(0)=1
, so
we get a decomposition

(80)

\Psi_{t}^{\rm fd}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\rm Im}(\sigma)}\Psi_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}).

A section of
\Psi_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
, as above, has
moderate growth
if all coefficients
c_{j}
are meromorphic, with poles at most along
(t=0)
.
We denote the subsheaves of such functions by
\Psi_{t,\alpha}^{m}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\subseteq\Psi_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
and

\Psi_{t}^{m}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\subseteq\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
. We also put
\Phi_{t}^{m}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})=\Psi_{t}^{m}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})/i^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm an}
.
Note that
\Psi_{t,\alpha}^{m}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
is filtered by
the subsheaves
\Psi_{t,\alpha,k}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
which only allow nonzero coefficients
c_{j}
for
j\leq k
. The natural morphism

i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}\to\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
factors through
\Psi_{t,1,k}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
for any
k\geq 1
.

Theorem 9.13
.

\big(
[
MaisonobeMebkhout
, Thm. 3.3-1]
\big)
For every regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, the natural morphisms

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Psi_{t,\alpha}^{m}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Psi_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Phi_{t}^{m}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\rm an},\Phi_{t}^{\rm fd}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)

are isomorphisms.

We can now prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
9.2
.

Applying Theorem
9.11
to
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and using the isomorphism (
79
), we
see that the following triangles are naturally isomorphic, where
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{M}^{*})^{\rm an}
:

i^{-1}{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})\to\Psi_{t}{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{\rm can}\Phi_{t}{\rm DR}_{X}^{\rm an}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{+1}\quad\text{and}\quad

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\Phi_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\xrightarrow{+1}.

Since the dual of a holonomic module is holonomic, hence coherent, we can apply Theorem
9.12
and we see that the latter triangle is isomorphic to

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\Psi^{\rm fd}_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\to{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\Phi^{\rm fd}_{t}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\xrightarrow{+1}.

Using the decomposition (
80
) and Theorem
9.13
, this triangle is isomorphic to

{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},i^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\rm Im}(\sigma)}{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\Psi^{m}_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)

\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\rm Im}(\sigma)}{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{i^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(i^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\Phi^{m}_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\big)\xrightarrow{+1}.

For
\alpha\notin\mathbf{Z}
, the second map in the above triangle is an isomorphism. In what follows, we mainly focus on the case
\alpha=1
,
which is the most interesting.

By definition of the sheaves
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha,k}
, we have an isomorphism
i^{-1}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}^{\rm an}_{\alpha,k})\cong\Psi_{t,\alpha,k}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
, hence

i^{-1}\varinjlim_{k}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}^{\rm an}_{\alpha,k})\cong\Psi^{m}_{t,\alpha}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
. Let
C_{k}^{\bullet}
be the complex
\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X}\to t^{*}(\mathcal{N}^{\rm an}_{1,k})
in the derived category of analytic
\mathcal{D}
-modules on
X
, with regular holonomic cohomology.
Then
i^{-1}\varinjlim_{k}C^{\bullet}_{k}=\Phi^{m}_{t,1}(\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})
. The above triangle, for
\alpha=1
, is thus isomorphic to

i^{-1}{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\rm an}_{X})\to i^{-1}{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}\big(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\varinjlim_{k}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{1,k})\big)\to i^{-1}{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}^{\rm an}_{X}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\varinjlim_{k}C^{\bullet}_{k})\xrightarrow{+1}.

By (the analytic version of)
[
HTT
, Lemma 2.6.13]
, which relates
-\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}-
and
\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{D}}(-,-)
, this triangle is isomorphic to

i^{-1}{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M})\to i^{-1}{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}\big(\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\varinjlim_{k}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{1,k})\big)\to i^{-1}{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{X}(\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\varinjlim_{k}C^{\bullet}_{k})\xrightarrow{+1}.

By the compatibility of the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence with pull-back
(see
[
HTT
, Equation 7.1.5]
), for every regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{N}
, we have

(81)

i^{-1}{\rm DR}_{X}(\mathcal{N})\cong{\rm DR}_{H}(\mathcal{N}|_{H}).

Hence, the above exact triangle is isomorphic to

{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}(\mathcal{M}|_{H})\to{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}\big((\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\varinjlim_{k}t^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{1,k}))|_{H}\big)\to{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}\big((\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\varinjlim_{k}C^{\bullet}_{k})|_{H}\big)\xrightarrow{+1}

which, in the notation we have been using, is

{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}(\mathcal{M}|_{H})\to{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}\big(\varinjlim_{k}(\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)\to{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}\big(\varinjlim_{k}(\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,k})|_{H}\big)\xrightarrow{+1}.

Finally, we have already shown that this is isomorphic to

{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}(\mathcal{M}|_{H})\to{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{1}(\mathcal{M})\big)\to{\rm DR}^{\rm an}_{H}\big({\rm Gr}_{V}^{0}(\mathcal{M})\big)\xrightarrow{+1},

proving the claim in the case
\alpha=1
. The case
\alpha\not\in{\mathbf{Z}}
is proved similarly and we leave it as an exercise for the reader.
∎

10.
The
V
-filtration on
B_{f}
and invariants of singularities

In this final section we discuss some connections between the invariants of singularities that we have seen so far (the roots of the Bernstein-Sato polynomial of
f
and the
V
-filtration
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
with respect to
f
) and other invariants of singularities.

10.1.
The reduced Bernstein-Sato polynomial

We first note that the Bernstein-Sato polynomial can be defined for any hypersurface. Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible algebraic variety over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
and let
H
be a hypersurface in
X
. We can choose a finite open cover
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}U_{i}
and
f_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(U_{i})
nonzero such that

H\cap U_{i}
is defined in
U_{i}
by
f_{i}
for all
i
. The
Bernstein-Sato
polynomial
b_{H}(s)
is defined as
{\rm lcm}\big\{b_{f_{i}}(s)\mid 1\leq i\leq r\big\}
.
It follows from Remarks
5.8
and
5.10
that the definition is independent of the choice of open cover and the
f_{i}
.

Proposition 10.1
.

If
H
is a nonempty hypersurface in
X
, then
(s+1)
divides
b_{H}(s)
.

Proof.

We may and will assume that
X
is affine and
H
is defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
noninvertible.
By definition, we have

b_{f}(s)f^{s}\in\mathcal{D}_{X}[s]ff^{s}.

After specializing to
s=-1
(see Remark
5.1
), we obtain

b_{f}(-1)\tfrac{1}{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot 1=\mathcal{O}_{X},

and since
f
is not invertible, we conclude that
b_{f}(-1)=0
.
∎

In light of this proposition, if
H
is nonempty, we write
b_{H}(s)=(s+1)\cdot\widetilde{b}_{H}(s)
; the polynomial
\widetilde{b}_{H}(s)
is the

reduced Bernstein-Sato polynomial
of
H
. If
H
is defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, then we also write
\widetilde{b}_{f}
for
\widetilde{b}_{H}
.

10.2.
The
V
-filtration and multiplier ideals

In this section we describe the connection between the
V
-filtration and the Bernstein-Sato polynomial, on one side, and multiplier ideals, on the other side. We begin with a quick introduction to multiplier ideals. For a more detailed discussion and for the proofs of some of the results we state, we refer to
[
Lazarsfeld
, Chapter 9]
.

Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible algebraic variety over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
. Suppose that
H
is a (nonempty) hypersurface on
X
.
We consider a log resolution
\pi\colon Y\to X
of the pair
(X,H)
.

Definition 10.2
.

For every
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
, the
multiplier ideal

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
is given by

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)=\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\big(K_{Y/X}-\lfloor\lambda\pi^{*}(H)\rfloor\big).

We note that, by definition, if
\pi^{*}(H)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}E_{i}
, then
\lfloor\lambda\pi^{*}(H)\rfloor=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lfloor\lambda a_{i}\rfloor E_{i}
. We also note that since
\lfloor\lambda\pi^{*}(H)\rfloor
is an effective
divisor and since
K_{Y/X}
is an effective exceptional divisor, we have

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)\subseteq\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(K_{Y/X})=\mathcal{O}_{X},

hence
\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
is indeed a coherent ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
It is a basic fact that the definition of multiplier ideals is independent of the choice of log resolution (see
[
Lazarsfeld
, Theorem 9.2.18]
).

In what follows we list a few properties of multiplier ideals. Most of these follow in a straightforward way from the definition:

1)

If
\lambda\geq\mu
, then

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)\subseteq\mathcal{J}(X,\mu H).

This is a consequence of the fact that the divisor
\lfloor\lambda\pi^{*}(H)\rfloor-\lfloor\mu\pi^{*}(H)\rfloor
is effective.

2)

It is an immediate consequence of the properties of the round-down function that for every
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
, there is
\epsilon>0

such that

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)=\mathcal{J}(X,\mu H)\quad\text{for}\quad\lambda\leq\mu\leq\lambda+\epsilon.

3)

In particular, we have
\mathcal{J}(X,\mu H)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for
0\leq\mu\ll 1
.

4)

We say that
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{>0}
is a
jumping number
of
(X,H)
if

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)\subsetneq\mathcal{J}\big(X,(\lambda-\epsilon)H\big)\quad\text{for all}\quad\epsilon>0.

Note that in this case we have
a_{i}\lambda\in{\mathbf{Z}}
for some
i
with
1\leq i\leq N
. Therefore the set of jumping numbers of
(X,H)
is contained in
\tfrac{1}{\ell}{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}

for some positive integer
\ell
.

5)

The smallest jumping number of
(X,H)
is the
log canonical threshold

\lct(X,H)

(also written
\lct(H)
when
X
is understood):

\lct(X,H)=\min\big\{\lambda>0\mid\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H\big)\neq\mathcal{O}_{X}\big\}.

Note that, using the notation in (
35
),
we have
1\in\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
if and only if
k_{i}\geq\lfloor\lambda a_{i}\rfloor
for all
i
; equivalently,

k_{i}>\lambda a_{i}-1
for all
i
. We thus deduce that

(82)

\lct(X,H)=\min_{i}\tfrac{k_{i}+1}{a_{i}}.

6)

1
is always a jumping number of
(X,H)
. In order to see this, we may replace
X
by any open subset
U
such that
U\cap H\neq\emptyset
.
We may thus assume that
H=mZ
for some smooth hypersurface
Z
and some positive integer
m
. In this case we may take
\pi
to be the identity and we see that

\mathcal{J}(X,H)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(-mZ)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{J}\big(X,(1-\epsilon)H\big)=\mathcal{O}_{X}\big(-(m-1)Z\big)\,\,\text{for}\,\,0<\epsilon\ll 1.

7)

For every
\lambda\geq 1
, it follows from the definition and the projection formula that

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)=\mathcal{O}_{X}(-H)\cdot\mathcal{J}\big(X,(\lambda-1)H\big).

In particular, we see that
\lambda>1
is a jumping number of
(X,H)
if and only if
\lambda-1
has this property.
This means that as invariants of singularities, it is enough to consider the multiplier ideals
\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
for
\lambda<1
.

8)

If
k={\mathbf{C}}
, then there is an analytic description of
\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
that is more intuitive than the algebraic one that we gave. Suppose, for simplicity,
that
H
is defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. In this case we have

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)=\big\{g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\mid\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\lambda}}\,\,\text{is locally integrable}\big\}.

The local integrability condition means that for every
P\in X
, if
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
are local coordinates around
P
, then there is an open neighborhood
U

of
P
such that
\int_{U}\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\lambda}}dzd\overline{z}<\infty
.
The equivalence with the formula in the algebraic definition is shown using the Change of Variable formula and the fact that, in one variable,

\tfrac{1}{|z|^{2\lambda}}dzd\overline{z}
is locally integrable if and only if
\lambda<1
(see
[
Lazarsfeld
, Chapter 9.3.D]
for details).

The following 3 results relate the
\mathcal{D}
-module theoretic invariants of
f
to the multiplier ideals of
H
.
The description of the log canonical threshold in the following theorem was proved by Kollár
[
Kollar
]
, by making use of Lichtin’s upper bound
[
Lichtin
]

for the roots of the Bernstein-Sato
polynomial that appears in Theorem
5.23
i).

Theorem 10.3
.

The largest root of
b_{H}(s)
is
-\lct(X,H)
.

Partially generalizing this to higher jumping numbers, we have the following result due to Ein, Lazarsfeld, Smith, and Varolin
[
ELSV
]
.

Theorem 10.4
.

If
\lambda\leq 1
is a jumping number of
(X,H)
, then
b_{H}(-\lambda)=0
.

As we will see, both the above results follow from the following theorem of Budur and Saito
[
BudurSaito
]
, that describes the multiplier ideals of
(X,H)

via the
V
-filtration of
f
, when
H
is defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. Recall that
\iota\colon X\hookrightarrow X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
is the graph embedding associated to
f
.

Theorem 10.5
.

For every
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)=\big\{g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}\mid g\delta\in V^{>\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\big\}.

The proof of this result in
[
BudurSaito
]
makes use of results in Saito’s theory of mixed Hodge modules
[
Saito-MHM
]
. Here we give a more elementary proof
following
[
DM
]
.

Proof of Theorem
10.5
.

We may and will assume that
X
is affine, with
R=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
and let
\pi\colon Y\to X
be a log resolution of
(X,H)
. Let
g\in R
be nonzero. We use the notation in (
35
) and denote by

b_{i}
the coefficient of
E_{i}
in
\pi^{*}\big({\rm div}(g)\big)
. By definition, we have
g\in\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
if and only if

b_{i}+k_{i}\geq\lfloor\lambda a_{i}\rfloor
for all
i
, which is the case if and only if
\lambda<\min_{i}\tfrac{b_{i}+k_{i}+1}{a_{i}}=:{\rm lct}_{g}(X,H)
.
On the other hand, it follows from Corollary
5.15
that
g\delta\in V^{>\lambda}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
if and only if all roots of
b_{g\delta}
are
<-\lambda
.
We thus conclude that the assertion in the theorem is equivalent to the fact that for every nonzero
g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, the largest root of
b_{g\delta}
is
-\lct_{g}(X,H)
.

The fact that every root of
b_{g\delta}
is
\leq-\lct_{g}(X,H)
follows from Theorem
5.23
iii). In order to complete the proof,
it is enough to show that if
g\in\mathcal{J}\big(X,(\lambda-\epsilon)H\big)
for every
\epsilon>0
, but

g\not\in\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
, then
b_{g\delta}(-\lambda)=0
.
For this,
we use the Lefschetz Principle to reduce to the
case when the ground field is
{\mathbf{C}}
, when we can use the analytic description of multiplier ideals.
The argument follows closely the argument for the proof of Theorem
10.4
in
[
ELSV
]
.

We may assume that we have coordinates
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
on
X
.
Since
g\not\in\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
, it follows that there is a point
x_{0}\in X
such that
\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\lambda}}
is not integrable in any neighborhood of
x_{0}
.
On the other hand, since
\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\mu}}
is locally integrable at
x_{0}
for all
\mu<\lambda
, we can choose an open ball
B
around
x_{0}
(with respect to our coordinates)
such that
\int_{B}\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\mu}}dzd\overline{z}<\infty
for all
\mu<\lambda
(the fact that we can choose
B
independently of
\mu
follows from the proof of the analytic characterization
of the multiplier ideal, see
[
Lazarsfeld
, Chapter 9.3.D]
).

By definition of the
b
-function, if we put
b=b_{g\delta}
, then there is
P\in D_{R}[s]
such that

b(s)gf^{s}=P\cdot gf^{s+1}.

Suppose that we are in an open subset
U
of
X^{\rm an}
where a branch of
{\rm log}(f)
is defined, hence
f^{\mu}={\rm exp}\big(\mu\cdot{\rm log}(f)\big)
is defined
for every
\mu\in{\mathbf{R}}
. We choose
\mu
such that
0<\lambda-\mu\ll 1

and specialize to
s=-\mu
(see Remark
5.1
), to get

b(-\mu)gf^{-\mu}=P(-\mu)\cdot gf^{1-\mu}.

Applying complex conjugation, we obtain

b(-\mu)\overline{g}\overline{f}^{-\mu}=\overline{P}(-\mu)\overline{g}\overline{f}^{1-\mu}.

Using the fact that that

P\cdot(\overline{h_{1}}h_{2})=\overline{h_{1}}P\cdot h_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{P}\cdot(\overline{h_{1}}h_{2})=h_{2}\overline{P}\cdot\overline{h_{1}}

for every holomorphic functions
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, we conclude that if
R=P\overline{P}
, then

(83)

b(-\mu)^{2}\cdot\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\mu}}=R(-\mu)\cdot\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2(\mu-1)}}.

Note that this formula does not depend on
U
and it makes sense on
X\smallsetminus H
.

Since both sides of (
83
) are integrable on
B
,
if
\varphi
is a smooth function with compact support on
B
, then the following integrals are finite

\int_{B}b(-\mu)^{2}\cdot\tfrac{|g|}{|f|^{2\mu}}\varphi dzd\overline{z}=\int_{B}\big(R(-\mu)\cdot\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2(\mu-1)}}\big)\varphi dzd\overline{z}=\int_{B}\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2(\mu-1)}}\psi dzd\overline{z},

where
\psi=\widetilde{R}(-\mu)
(here
\widetilde{R}
is the classical adjoint of
R
) and the last equality in the displayed formula is a consequence of the
Stokes Theorem. In particular, by choosing
\varphi
to be nonnegative and with
\varphi=1
on a smaller ball
B^{\prime}\subsetneq B
, we get

b(-\mu)^{2}\cdot\int_{B^{\prime}}\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\mu}}dzd\overline{z}\leq\int_{B}\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2(\mu-1)}}\psi dzd\overline{z}\leq M,

for some constant
M
that is independent of
\mu
. If
b(-\lambda)\neq 0
, then we conclude that
\int_{B^{\prime}}\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\mu}}dzd\overline{z}

is bounded for
\mu\to\lambda
, hence
\tfrac{|g|^{2}}{|f|^{2\lambda}}
is integrable on
B^{\prime}
by the monotone convergence theorem.
This contradiction completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 10.6

(The result of Kollár and Lichtin)
.

Suppose that
X
and
H
are as in Theorem
10.5
. We have seen in the proof of the theorem that its statement is equivalent to the fact that for every
g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
nonzero,
the largest root of
b_{g\delta}
is
-\lct_{g}(X,H)
. The special case
g=1
corresponds to the assertion in Theorem
10.3

Proof of Theorem
10.4
.

We may and will assume that
X
is an affine variety, with
R=\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, and
H
is defined by
f\in R
. Since
\lambda
is a jumping number, it follows that there is
g\in R
such that
g\in\mathcal{J}\big(X,(\lambda-\epsilon)H\big)

for
0<\epsilon\ll 1
, but
g\not\in\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)
. For simplicity, we write
V^{\alpha}
for
V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
By Theorem
10.5
, we have
g\delta\in V^{\lambda}\smallsetminus V^{>\lambda}
.
On the other hand, by definition of
b_{f}
, there is
P\in D_{R}[s]
such that

b_{f}(s)\delta=P\cdot t\delta.

Note that
\delta\in V^{>0}
: this follows from Theorem
10.5
since
\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for
\lambda=0
; alternatively, it follows from the description
of the
V
-filtration in Proposition
5.15
and the fact that the roots of
b_{f}
are negative by Theorem
5.23
i). Therefore
t\delta\in V^{>1}\subseteq V^{>\lambda}
. Since
b_{f}(s)g\delta=gP\cdot t\delta\in V^{>\lambda}
, it follows that
b_{f}(s)
annihilates a nonzero element in
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\lambda}
.
Since
(s+\lambda)^{N}
annihilates
{\rm Gr}_{V}^{\lambda}
for some
N
, it follows that
{\rm gcd}\big(b_{f},(s+\lambda)^{N}\big)
annihilates a nonzero element, hence

(s+\lambda)
divides
b_{f}
.
∎

10.3.
The
V
-filtration and the minimal exponent

Our goal in this section is to discuss a refinement of the notions of log canonical threshold and multiplier ideals due to Saito.
The idea is to use the
V
-filtration in order to define a version of multiplier ideals that give new information also for
\lambda\geq 1
.

We fix a smooth, irreducible algebraic variety

X
and a (nonempty) hypersurface
H
in
X
. To begin with, we assume that
H
is defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. For simplicity, we write

V^{\alpha}
for
V^{\alpha}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
, where
\iota\colon X\hookrightarrow X\times{\mathbf{A}}^{1}
is the graph embedding corresponding to
f
.

Definition 10.7
.

For every
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
, we write
\lambda=\alpha+q
, where
q\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\alpha\in[0,1)
, and we denote by

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)
the coherent ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
consisting of those
h\in\mathcal{O}_{X}
with the property that there are
h_{0},\ldots,h_{q-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}

such that
h_{0}\delta+\ldots+h_{q-1}\partial_{t}^{q-1}\delta+h\partial_{t}^{q}\delta\in V^{>\alpha}
.

These ideals have been introduced by Saito in
[
Saito_Hodge
]
as
microlocal multiplier ideals
, due to the fact that the definition
was expressed in terms of the so-called
microlocal
V
-filtration
(however, we will not use this terminology). Under the name of
higher multiplier ideals

and with a different indexing, the ideals have been studied systematically by Schnell and Yang in their recent preprint
[
SchnellYang
]
using twisted Hodge modules.

Remark 10.8

(The connection with the classical version)
.

It is a consequence of Theorem
10.5
that for
\lambda<1
, we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)=\mathcal{J}(X,\lambda H).

Remark 10.9

(Independence of the equation)
.

If
g\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
defines the same hypersurface, then we can write
g=pf
, for some invertible
p\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. If
\iota_{f}
and
\iota_{g}
are the graph embeddings
corresponding to
f
and
g
, respectively, then it follows from Remark
5.10
that

V^{>\alpha}(\iota_{g})_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\big\{\sum_{j=0}^{q}p^{j+1}u_{j}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\mid q\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0},\,\,\sum_{j=0}^{q}u_{j}\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\in V^{>\alpha}(\iota_{f})_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\big\}.

This immediately implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)
does not depend on the choice of
f
. We can thus define
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)
for every hypersurface
H
,
by glueing the corresponding ideals on a suitable open cover such that
H
is a principal divisor in each of these open subsets.

Remark 10.10

(Discreteness and right continuity of the ideals
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)
)
.

For every
H
, there is a positive integer
\ell
such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)
is constant
for
\lambda\in\big[\tfrac{i}{\ell},\tfrac{i+1}{\ell}\big)
for every
i\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
. Indeed, it is enough to check this when
H
is defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, in which case
the assertion follows from the fact that

V^{\bullet}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
is discrete and left continuous.
In particular, we see for every
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
, there is
\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda
such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)=\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\mu H)

for every
\lambda\leq\mu\leq\lambda^{\prime}
.

Proposition 10.11
.

For every hypersurface
H
on the smooth, irreducible variety
X
and every
\lambda\geq\mu
, we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\mu H).

Proof.

We may assume that
X
is affine and
H
is defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
.
It follows directly from the definition that for every
q\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\mu H)

for
q\leq\mu\leq\lambda<q+1
. Therefore, in order to prove the proposition, it is enough to show that
for every
q\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}
and every
\mu
, with
q-1\leq\mu<q
, we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,qH)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}\big(X,\mu H).

In order to prove this, let
h
be a global section of
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,qH)
, hence there are
h_{0},\ldots,h_{q-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)

such that
u=h_{0}\delta+\ldots+h_{q-1}\partial_{t}^{q-1}\delta+h\partial_{t}^{q}\delta\in V^{>0}
.
Note that
\delta\in V^{>0}
: this follows, using Proposition
5.15
, from the fact that
b_{f}
has negative roots, see
Theorem
5.23
i). Therefore we have

\partial_{t}\cdot(h_{1}\delta+\ldots+h\partial_{t}^{q-1}\delta)\in V^{>0}\subseteq V^{0}.

We deduce from Corollary
3.12
ii) that
h_{1}\delta+\ldots+h\partial_{t}^{q-1}\delta\in V^{1}
, hence
h\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\mu H)

for every
\mu
, with
q-1\leq\mu<q
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 10.12
.

Let
X
be a smooth, irreducible variety and
H
the hypersurface defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
. If
\lambda\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
is written as
\lambda=\alpha+q
,
where
q\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\alpha\in[0,1)
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
if and only if
\partial_{t}^{q}\delta\in V^{>\alpha}
.

Proof.

The “if” part is clear from the definition, so we only need to prove the converse. We argue by induction on
q
, the case
q=0
being clear.
We may and will assume that
X
is affine. Suppose that
1\in\Gamma\big(X,\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)\big)
, so there are

h_{0},\ldots,h_{q-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
such that

(84)

h_{0}\delta+\ldots+h_{q-1}\partial_{t}^{q-1}\delta+\partial_{t}^{q}\delta\in V^{>\alpha}.

By Proposition
10.11
, we know that
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}\big(X,(\lambda-i)H\big)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for
1\leq i\leq q
, hence
by induction we know that
\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\in V^{>\alpha}
for
j<q
. We thus deduce from (
84
) that

\partial_{t}^{q}\delta\in V^{>\alpha}
, completing the proof of the induction step.
∎

Example 10.13

(The ideals
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)
when
H
is smooth)
.

If
H
is a smooth hypersurface of
X
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for all
\lambda\geq 0
. Indeed, it follows from
Remark
4.7
that

\partial_{t}^{j}\delta\in V^{1}\iota_{+}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
for all
j\geq 0
.
In fact, the converse also holds: if
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(X,\lambda H)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for all
\lambda\geq 0
, then
H
is smooth.
However, the proof of this assertion requires tools beyond the ones we discuss in these notes:
the condition
